well-defined modulo zero \(L^2\)-classes on the dense program algebra. It extends uniquely to

\[
\Gamma:L^2_R\to L^2_L.
\]

Exact finite-dimensional duality gives

\[
\langle\Gamma v,w\rangle_L
=
\langle v,\Gamma^*w\rangle_R
\]

on dense algebras, so the formal transpose extends to the genuine Hilbert adjoint.

This repairs the probability spaces, nonlinear functional calculus, bounded action, and adjoint. It is a substantial missing lemma, not a cosmetic clarification.

## 3. The Picard argument is not valid in the stated Banach space

The estimate

\[
\|Cq(S)-\widetilde Cq(\widetilde S)\|_2
\le
\|C-\widetilde C\|_2
+
\|\widetilde C\|_\infty
\operatorname{Lip}(q)\|S-\widetilde S\|_2
\]

uses an \(L^\infty\) bound on \(C\). Thus the vector field is not locally Lipschitz on an open subset of

\[
L^2_R\times L^2_L\times\mathfrak S_1.
\]

### Repair

Perform the fixed-point argument on the closed path set

\[
\mathcal B_{R,B}
=
\left\{
(X,C,M):
\sup_{t\le\tau}\|(X,C,M)-(X_0,C_0,M_0)\|\le R,\ 
|C(t)|\le B\ \text{a.e.}
\right\},
\]

using the uniform \(L^2_R\times L^2_L\times\mathfrak S_1\) path metric. This set is complete.

On it,

\[
\|H-\widetilde H\|_2\le\|X-\widetilde X\|_2,
\]

\[
\|S-\widetilde S\|_2
\le
(\|M_0\|_{\rm op}+R)\|X-\widetilde X\|_2
+
\|H\|_2\|M-\widetilde M\|_{\mathfrak S_1},
\]

and the preceding estimate controls \(D-\widetilde D\). Finally,

\[
\|D\otimes H-\widetilde D\otimes\widetilde H\|_{\mathfrak S_1}
\le
\|D-\widetilde D\|_2\|H\|_2+
\|\widetilde D\|_2\|H-\widetilde H\|_2.
\]

Hence the integral map is a contraction for sufficiently small \(\tau\). It preserves the \(L^\infty\) envelope because \(|\arctan S|\le\pi/2\).

For the resulting local solution, the pointwise/Fubini Sobolev chain rule yields

\[
\dot F=-2eK,\qquad
e(t)=-\exp\left(-2\int_0^tK(s)\,ds\right).
\]

Therefore

\[
|e(t)|\le1,\qquad
\|C(t)\|_\infty\le\pi t,
\]

\[
\|\dot M(t)\|_{\mathfrak S_1}
\le 4\left(\frac\pi2\right)^2t,
\]

and \(X\) also remains bounded in \(L^2\) on compact intervals. These bounds give global continuation.

Thus the IDE is well posed after this repair.

## 4. The correct law-level state

A bare Hilbert-operator state is not enough, because arbitrary Hilbert unitaries need not preserve pointwise nonlinearities.

The correct state is the isomorphism class of

\[
(\Omega_R,\mu_R;\Omega_L,\mu_L;M,X,C),
\]

where

\[
M:L^2_R\to L^2_L
\]

is bounded and \(M-M_0\in\mathfrak S_1\). Equivalence must be by probability-algebra/Koopman isomorphisms \(U_R,U_L\) satisfying

\[
\widetilde M U_R=U_LM,\qquad
\widetilde X=U_RX,\qquad
\widetilde C=U_LC.
\]

These maps preserve expectations, pointwise nonlinearities, and rank-one updates:

\[
U_L(D\otimes H)U_R^{-1}
=
(U_LD)\otimes(U_RH).
\]

Using the current \(M_t\), rather than the decomposition \(\Gamma+Q_t\), the evolution is

\[
\dot X=-2eM^*D,\qquad
\dot C=-2eG,\qquad
\dot M=-2eD\otimes H.
\]

Its future depends only on the current \((M_t,X_t,C_t)\), so it is autonomous and restartable.

This is a very rich operator-valued law, but it is not a retained finite array or trajectory history. The prompt explicitly permits genuinely necessary conditional or edge laws, so this interpretation is admissible.

## 5. The original action topology is too strong

The submitted topology allowed arbitrary sums and polynomial products of probes. The available mesh estimate is only in \(L^2\) and trace norm. Such convergence does not propagate through arbitrary products.

The topology should instead contain:

- the current state \(X,C,M\);
- the dynamically required fields
  \[
  H,\ S,\ G,\ D,\ P=M^*D,
  \]
  \[
  q(U)P,\quad q(U)^2P,\quad
  M(q(U)^2P);
  \]
- bounded Lipschitz/truncated cylinder probes;
- the separate second moments needed for kernels and velocities.

This still determines the entire exact vector field and all requested observables, while avoiding unjustified higher-product closure.

## 6. Uniform integrability and reused-adjoint convergence were only sketched

Path-\(W_2\) convergence does not imply convergence of derivatives or kernels. The delicate field is

\[
P_n(t)=M_n(t)^*D_n(t).
\]

Fix a coarse auxiliary mesh \(\delta\), and let \(P_n^\delta\) be the corresponding piecewise-constant Euler field. Uniform same-width stability gives

\[
\sup_{t\le T}\|P_n(t)-P_n^\delta(t)\|_n
\le C_T\delta.
\tag{1}
\]

For fixed \(\delta\), only finitely many Euler states occur. The fixed-program theorem applied to \(|P|^p\), \(p>2\), yields a uniform-in-those-finitely-many-times \(p\)-moment bound in probability.

The elementary tail inequality gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
\sup_{t\le T}
\left\langle
P_n(t)^2\mathbf 1_{\{|P_n(t)|>R\}}
\right\rangle_n
\le {}&
2\sup_{t\le T}
\left\langle
P_n^\delta(t)^2
\mathbf 1_{\{|P_n^\delta(t)|>R/2\}}
\right\rangle_n\\
&+4C_T^2\delta^2.
\end{aligned}
\tag{2}
\]

First fix \(\delta\), then let \(n\to\infty\), then \(R\to\infty\). Equation (2) leaves at most \(4C_T^2\delta^2\); sending \(\delta\downarrow0\) proves

\[
\lim_{R\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}
\Pr\!\left(
\sup_{t\le T}
\left\langle
P_n(t)^2\mathbf1_{\{|P_n(t)|>R\}}
\right\rangle_n>\varepsilon
\right)=0.
\tag{3}
\]

No moment estimate uniform in \(\delta\) is needed.

Now truncate \(P_n\). On \(|P_n|\le R\), Lipschitzness of \(q\circ J\) and \(L^2\)-convergence of \(X_n\) give convergence of

\[
q(U_n)^aP_n,\qquad a=1,2.
\]

The tail is controlled by (3). Thus these fields converge in \(L^2\), uniformly in time in probability.

For the reused forward action,

\[
p_n=q(U_n)^2P_n,
\]

and

\[
\|M_np_n-M_n^\delta p_n^\delta\|_2
\le
\|M_n-M_n^\delta\|_{\rm op}\|p_n\|_2
+
\|M_n^\delta\|_{\rm op}\|p_n-p_n^\delta\|_2.
\]

Both terms vanish after the preceding truncation argument. At fixed \(\delta\), \(M_n^\delta p_n^\delta\) is again a finite Tensor-Program probe.

This closes convergence of

\[
K^{(1)}=\|q(U)P\|_2^2
\]

and of

\[
\dot S
=
-2e\left(
\|H\|_2^2D+M(q(U)^2P)
\right).
\]

## 7. Exact polygonal-GD velocities

Inside the \(k\)-th polygonal GD interval,

\[
\dot u=-2e_kq(u_k)P_k,
\]

and

\[
\dot S
=
-2e_k\left[
D_k\langle H_k,H(t)\rangle_n
+
M(t)\bigl(q(u(t))q(u_k)P_k\bigr)
\right].
\tag{4}
\]

The uniform bounds give

\[
\|u(t)-u_k\|_\infty
\le C_T\eta_n\sqrt n,
\qquad
\|M(t)-M_k\|_{\rm op}\le C_T\eta_n.
\]

For \(\eta_n=n^{-2}\), both vanish. Combining (3)–(4) proves the required \(L^1(0,T)\) convergence of \(V_n^{(1)2}\) and \(V_n^{(2)2}\). Therefore the time-integrated velocity claims are repairable and valid.

## 8. Small-time expansion

The displayed coefficients are correct, but the original proof silently treated Nemytskii maps as Fréchet differentiable \(L^2\to L^2\), which need not hold.

The needed replacement is the following directional lemma. If

\[
\frac{r(t)}{t^k}\to r\quad\text{in }L^2
\]

and \(F\in C^1\) has bounded continuous derivative, then

\[
\frac{F(X+r(t))-F(X)}{t^k}
\to F'(X)r
\quad\text{in }L^2.
\]

Indeed, \(r(t)/t^k\to r\) in \(L^2\) makes their squares uniformly integrable; the mean-value remainder has a bounded factor converging to zero in probability, so Vitali convergence applies.

Applying this successively to \(J,\Psi,\arctan\) justifies the expansions and hence

\[
K(t)=k_3+8(d_0+m\nu)t^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
V^{(1)}(t)^2=16d_0t^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
V^{(2)}(t)^2
=
16\|\mathcal RY\|_2^2t^2+o(t^2),
\]

with strictly positive coefficients. The nondegeneracy, nonlinearity, loss decrease, and kernel movement conclusions therefore survive.

## Final verdict

- The proof as originally stated: **invalid/incomplete**.
- The proposed scaling and limiting mechanism: **not refuted**.
- The finite-width algebra, exact-GD diagonal estimate, and local nonlinear feature-learning coefficients: **correct**.
- The foundational and uniform-integrability gaps: **repairable as above**.
- After replacing the action construction, Picard argument, topology, and velocity/UI passage by the repaired lemmas, I find no remaining mathematical obstruction to the affirmative theorem.

So the prior answer should be treated as a promising proof skeleton, not a completed theorem. The corrected operator-law theorem is viable, but only with the substantial repairs above explicitly included.

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 22443 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.292Z | assistant | final_answer ===
Yes. After replacing the overstrong polynomial-probe topology and repairing the probability-space construction, the arctangent construction gives a rigorous affirmative example.

No project files were inspected. The only external ingredient is the fixed-finite-program Tensor Program Master Theorem, whose exact scope was checked from the primary source.

## Theorem

Let

\[
\phi(x)=\arctan x
\]

and choose

\[
\sigma_{1,n}^{2}=1,\qquad
\sigma_{2,n}^{2}=\frac1n,\qquad
\sigma_{3,n}^{2}=\frac1{n^{4}},
\]

\[
\lambda_n^{(1)}=n,\qquad
\lambda_n^{(2)}=1,\qquad
\lambda_n^{(3)}=\frac1n,
\qquad
\eta_{0,n}=n^{-2}.
\]

Then

\[
\eta_{0,n}\lambda_n^{(1)}=n^{-1},\qquad
\eta_{0,n}\lambda_n^{(2)}=n^{-2},\qquad
\eta_{0,n}\lambda_n^{(3)}=n^{-3},
\]

so every raw layerwise step tends to zero.

There exists \(T_*>0\) and a deterministic, autonomous operator-law gradient flow such that, for every \(0<T<T_*\), the polygonally interpolated exact GD trajectory converges in probability, along the full sequence \(n\to\infty\), uniformly on \([0,T]\). The convergence includes:

- the complete dynamically reachable operator action;
- both hidden path laws in \(W_2(C([0,T]))\);
- all three parameter updates;
- the output, loss, and all kernel blocks uniformly in time;
- both feature velocities in the time-integrated topology required in the question.

Every one of properties 1–7 holds on every such interval.

---

# 1. Exact finite-width equations

Write

\[
u=W^{(1)},\qquad M=W^{(2)},\qquad c=nW^{(3)}
\]

and use

\[
\langle v,w\rangle_n=\frac1n v^\top w,\qquad
\|v\|_n^2=\langle v,v\rangle_n.
\]

For vectors on the two hidden layers define

\[
(a\otimes_n b)v=a\langle b,v\rangle_n=\frac1n ab^\top v.
\]

Set

\[
h=\phi(u),\qquad s=Mh,\qquad g=\phi(s),
\]

\[
q(x)=\phi'(x)=\frac1{1+x^2},\qquad
d=cq(s),
\]

where products are coordinatewise, and

\[
f=\langle c,g\rangle_n,\qquad e=f-1.
\]

Direct differentiation gives

\[
\nabla_u f=\frac1n q(u)M^\top d,
\]

\[
\nabla_M f=\frac1n dh^\top,
\]

\[
\nabla_{W^{(3)}}f=g.
\]

Consequently one exact GD step of duration \(\eta=\eta_{0,n}\) is

\[
u^+=u-2\eta e\,q(u)M^\top d,
\tag{1}
\]

\[
M^+=M-2\eta e\,d\otimes_n h,
\tag{2}
\]

\[
c^+=c-2\eta e\,g.
\tag{3}
\]

There is no approximation in (1)–(3).

The three kernel blocks are exactly

\[
K_n^{(1)}=\|q(u)M^\top d\|_n^2,
\tag{4}
\]

\[
K_n^{(2)}=\|h\|_n^2\|d\|_n^2,
\tag{5}
\]

\[
K_n^{(3)}=\|g\|_n^2.
\tag{6}
\]

Indeed, for example,

\[
n\|\nabla_u f\|_{\ell^2}^2
=
n\frac1{n^2}\sum_i(q(u_i)(M^\top d)_i)^2
=
K_n^{(1)},
\]

and the other two identities follow similarly.

The initialization is

\[
u_i(0)\sim N(0,1),\qquad
M_{ji}(0)\sim N(0,1/n),
\]

\[
c_j(0)=nW_j^{(3)}(0)\sim N(0,n^{-2}).
\tag{7}
\]

In particular,

\[
\|c(0)\|_n\longrightarrow0,\qquad
\|c(0)\|_\infty\longrightarrow0
\]

in probability.

---

# 2. The natural coordinate

Define

\[
\Theta(r)=r+\frac{r^3}{3},\qquad X=\Theta(u),\qquad J=\Theta^{-1}.
\]

Because

\[
\Theta'(r)=1+r^2=q(r)^{-1},
\]

the continuous version of (1) becomes

\[
\dot X=-2eM^*D.
\tag{8}
\]

Here and below uppercase letters denote limiting fields.

Put

\[
\Psi(x)=\arctan J(x).
\]

The required global estimates are

\[
|\,\arctan x\,|\le a:=\frac{\pi}{2},
\]

\[
\operatorname{Lip}(J)\le1,\qquad
\operatorname{Lip}(\Psi)\le1,
\]

\[
\operatorname{Lip}(q)=\frac{3\sqrt3}{8},
\qquad
\sup_x|x|q(x)^2=\frac{3\sqrt3}{16}.
\tag{9}
\]

The limiting equations will therefore be written as

\[
H=\Psi(X),\qquad S=MH,\qquad G=\arctan S,
\]

\[
D=Cq(S),\qquad F=\mathbb E_L[CG],\qquad e=F-1,
\]

\[
P=M^*D,
\]

\[
\boxed{
\dot X=-2eP,\qquad
\dot M=-2eD\otimes H,\qquad
\dot C=-2eG.
}
\tag{10}
\]

The rank-one operator means

\[
(D\otimes H)v=D\,\mathbb E_R[Hv].
\]

In the original coordinate \(U=J(X)\),

\[
\dot U=-2e\,q(U)M^*D.
\tag{11}
\]

---

# 3. Fixed-program theorem and the Gaussian operator

The only external theorem used is the following specialization.

### Fixed-program master theorem

Consider one fixed finite straight-line program containing:

- iid matrices with entries \(N(0,\sigma^2/n)\);
- iid-coordinate jointly Gaussian initial vectors;
- scalar inputs converging almost surely to deterministic limits;
- repeated multiplications by any initial matrix and its transpose;
- coordinatewise and empirical-moment maps that are jointly pseudo-Lipschitz in all vector and scalar arguments.

Then for every fixed finite tuple of generated vectors and every pseudo-Lipschitz test function, its empirical average converges almost surely to a deterministic master expectation. Every generated scalar converges almost surely as well.

This is precisely Setup G.2, Assumption L.4, and Theorem G.4 in the [Tensor Programs IV supplement](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/yang21c/yang21c-supp.pdf). The theorem is explicitly for a fixed finite program; we never apply it to a program whose length grows with \(n\). Reuse of \(M_0,M_0^\top\) is part of the MatMul language. Under Assumption L.4 no rank-stability hypothesis is imposed; singular limiting Gram matrices are permitted by the underlying master theorem.

All functions used here satisfy its assumptions:

- \(\Theta\) is pseudo-Lipschitz;
- \(J,\Psi,\arctan,q\) are globally Lipschitz;
- \((c,s)\mapsto cq(s)\), scalar-vector multiplication, and the finite products used below are pseudo-Lipschitz;
- the initial readout is encoded exactly as
  \[
  C_{0,n}=a_nR_n,\qquad a_n=\frac1n,\quad R_n\sim N(0,I_n),
  \]
  where \(a_n\to0\) and \((a,r)\mapsto ar\) is jointly pseudo-Lipschitz.

### Construction of the two-sided action

Create a countable, typed universal program as follows.

There are right and left variables. Start with the right Gaussian \(U_0\), the left auxiliary Gaussian \(R_0\), constants, and the matrix \(M_{0,n}\). Repeatedly add:

1. rational linear combinations;
2. a countable dense family of bounded Lipschitz cylinder functions;
3. the functions \(\Theta,J,\Psi,\arctan,q\);
4. \(M_{0,n}v\) for every constructed right variable;
5. \(M_{0,n}^{\top}w\) for every constructed left variable;
6. all instructions appearing in every rational-mesh, finite-step Euler scheme.

Dovetailing these instructions produces a countable sequence in which every finite prefix is a valid finite Tensor Program.

For each finite collection of right variables, apply the master theorem to obtain its deterministic limiting joint law. These laws are projectively consistent: extending a finite program leaves every old finite-\(n\) vector literally unchanged, so the same empirical bounded-Lipschitz statistic cannot have two different deterministic limits. The same construction is made for the left variables.

Kolmogorov extension therefore gives two countably generated probability spaces

\[
(\Omega_R,\mathcal F_R,\mathbb P_R),
\qquad
(\Omega_L,\mathcal F_L,\mathbb P_L).
\]

Let

\[
\mathcal H_R=L^2(\Omega_R),\qquad
\mathcal H_L=L^2(\Omega_L).
\]

The bounded-Lipschitz cylinder fields are dense. Indeed, conditional expectations onto the first \(m\) coordinate variables converge in \(L^2\), and continuous compactly supported functions are dense in each finite-dimensional \(L^2\) law.

For the initial Gaussian matrices, a \(1/4\)-net argument gives

\[
\mathbb P(\|M_{0,n}\|_{\mathrm{op}}>10)
\le
2\cdot81^n e^{-25n/2}.
\tag{12}
\]

Hence, almost surely, \(\|M_{0,n}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le10\) eventually.

On the dense right cylinder class define

\[
\Gamma v:=Z^{M_0v}.
\]

If \(v=\widetilde v\) in limiting \(L^2\), then by the master theorem and (12),

\[
\|\Gamma v-\Gamma\widetilde v\|_2^2
=
\lim_n\|M_{0,n}(v_n-\widetilde v_n)\|_n^2
\le
100\|v-\widetilde v\|_2^2.
\]

Thus \(\Gamma\) is well-defined and extends uniquely to

\[
\Gamma:\mathcal H_R\longrightarrow\mathcal H_L,
\qquad
\|\Gamma\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le10.
\]

The reverse action similarly defines a bounded operator \(\Gamma^\dagger\). Exact finite-dimensional duality gives, first on dense cylinder classes,

\[
\mathbb E_L[(\Gamma v)w]
=
\mathbb E_R[v(\Gamma^\dagger w)].
\]

Density implies

\[
\Gamma^\dagger=\Gamma^*.
\tag{13}
\]

This is the exact reused-adjoint identity required by the problem. It is obtained from the finite matrix identity, not postulated.

The auxiliary mark \(R_0\) disappears because \(a_nR_n\to0\). It is not part of the limiting state.

---

# 4. The autonomous law state

A limiting state is the isomorphism class of

\[
(\Omega_R,\Omega_L;X,C,M),
\]

where

\[
X\in L^2_R,\qquad C\in L^2_L\cap L^\infty_L,
\]

and

\[
M:\mathcal H_R\to\mathcal H_L
\]

is bounded, with \(M-\Gamma\) trace-class in the initial chart.

Two states are identified only under probability-algebra isomorphisms whose Koopman maps preserve \(X,C\) and intertwine \(M\). Arbitrary Hilbert-space unitaries are not allowed, because they need not preserve pointwise nonlinear functional calculus.

The actual restart state stores only the current \(M,X,C\). Neither the list of previous rank-one updates, nor a past trajectory, nor Euler syntax is retained. Equation (10) depends only on the current state, so uniqueness below proves restartability.

### Required-probe topology

The earlier topology allowing arbitrary products of arbitrary unbounded probes was too strong. The correct topology is the countable initial topology generated by:

- bounded-Lipschitz cylinder functions;
- repeated applications of \(M\) and \(M^*\);
- the dynamically required fields
  \[
  U,H,S,G,C,D,P,
  \]
- the three additional \(L^2\) fields
  \[
  q(U)P,\qquad q(U)^2P,\qquad M(q(U)^2P);
  \]
- their same-side finite joint \(W_2\) laws and the scalar expectations/norms appearing in the vector field and kernels.

If these probes are enumerated by \(r\), define

\[
d_{\mathrm{req}}(\mathsf S,\widetilde{\mathsf S})
=
\sum_{r\ge1}2^{-r}
\left(1\wedge W_2(\mu_r,\widetilde\mu_r)\right)
+
\sum_{r\ge1}2^{-r}
\left(1\wedge|\theta_r-\widetilde\theta_r|\right).
\tag{14}
\]

After quotienting by zero distance this is a genuine law-level metric. It determines every term of (10), all layer updates, both hidden laws, every kernel block, output, and loss.

---

# 5. Well-posedness of the operator flow

Write

\[
M=\Gamma+Q,\qquad Q\in\mathfrak S_1.
\]

Use the norm

\[
\rho((X,C,Q),(\widetilde X,\widetilde C,\widetilde Q))
=
\|X-\widetilde X\|_2+
\|C-\widetilde C\|_2+
\|Q-\widetilde Q\|_{\mathfrak S_1}.
\tag{15}
\]

Assume on a bounded set that

\[
\|\Gamma\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le B,\qquad
\|Q\|_1,\|\widetilde Q\|_1\le R,
\qquad
\|C\|_\infty,\|\widetilde C\|_\infty\le c.
\]

Put

\[
m=B+R,\qquad E=1+ac,\qquad
\ell=\frac{3\sqrt3}{8},
\]

\[
d_*=1+c\ell(m+a),
\]

\[
e_*=a+c(m+a),
\]

\[
p_*=md_*+c.
\]

Then

\[
\|H-\widetilde H\|_2\le\|X-\widetilde X\|_2,
\]

\[
\|S-\widetilde S\|_2
\le
m\|X-\widetilde X\|_2
+
a\|Q-\widetilde Q\|_1,
\]

\[
\|D-\widetilde D\|_2\le d_*\rho,
\]

\[
|e-\widetilde e|\le e_*\rho,
\]

\[
\|P-\widetilde P\|_2\le p_*\rho.
\]

Moreover,

\[
\|D\otimes H-\widetilde D\otimes\widetilde H\|_1
\le
(a d_*+c)\rho.
\]

Therefore the vector field in (10) is Lipschitz with, for example,

\[
\begin{aligned}
L(B,R,c)
={}&
2(Ep_*+e_*mc)\\
&+2(E(m+a)+ae_*)\\
&+2(E(ad_*+c)+ace_*).
\end{aligned}
\tag{16}
\]

Its norm is bounded by

\[
A(B,R,c)=2E(mc+a+ac).
\tag{17}
\]

A direct Picard theorem on an open \(L^2\) set would be invalid because the estimate for \(Cq(S)\) uses an \(L^\infty\) envelope. Instead, use the closed complete path set

\[
\sup_{t\le\tau}\rho(Y(t),Y(0))\le1,
\qquad
\sup_{t\le\tau}\|C(t)\|_\infty\le c_0+1.
\]

For sufficiently small \(\tau\), the integral map preserves both bounds because \(|G|\le a\), and it is a contraction by (16). An \(L^2\)-limit of functions uniformly bounded by \(c_0+1\) retains the same almost-everywhere bound, so the set is complete.

Thus (10) has a unique local solution.

### Energy identity and global existence

From \(U=J(X)\),

\[
\dot H=q(U)\dot U=-2e\,q(U)^2P.
\]

Hence

\[
\dot S
=
-2e\left[
\|H\|_2^2D+
M(q(U)^2P)
\right].
\tag{18}
\]

Using \(D=Cq(S)\),

\[
\begin{aligned}
\dot F
&=
\langle\dot C,G\rangle_L+
\langle C,q(S)\dot S\rangle_L\\
&=
-2e\left[
\|G\|_2^2+
\|H\|_2^2\|D\|_2^2+
\|q(U)P\|_2^2
\right].
\end{aligned}
\tag{19}
\]

Thus

\[
\dot e=-2Ke,
\]

where

\[
K=K^{(1)}+K^{(2)}+K^{(3)}
\]

and

\[
K^{(1)}=\|q(U)P\|_2^2,
\]

\[
K^{(2)}=\|H\|_2^2\|D\|_2^2,
\]

\[
K^{(3)}=\|G\|_2^2.
\tag{20}
\]

Consequently,

\[
|e(t)|\le |e(0)|=:E_0.
\]

Since \(|G|\le a\),

\[
\|C(t)\|_\infty
\le
\|C(0)\|_\infty+2aE_0t.
\tag{21}
\]

Also,

\[
\|Q(t)-Q(0)\|_1
\le
2aE_0
\left(
\|C(0)\|_\infty t+aE_0t^2
\right).
\tag{22}
\]

Finally,

\[
\|\dot X(t)\|_2
\le
2E_0\|M(t)\|_{\mathrm{op}}
\bigl(\|C(0)\|_\infty+2aE_0t\bigr).
\]

These estimates exclude finite-time blowup. The solution is global.

### It is genuinely a gradient flow

On

\[
L^2_R\times(\Gamma+\mathfrak S_2)\times L^2_L
\]

define

\[
F(U,M,C)=
\left\langle
C,\arctan(M\arctan U)
\right\rangle_L.
\]

Although the Nemytskii map \(U\mapsto\arctan U\) need not be Fréchet \(L^2\to L^2\), this scalar functional is Fréchet differentiable.

The needed scalar lemma is: if \(\varphi'\) is bounded and uniformly continuous, then for fixed \(z,p\in L^2\),

\[
\left\langle
p,\varphi(z+h)-\varphi(z)-\varphi'(z)h
\right\rangle
=o(\|h\|_2).
\tag{23}
\]

To prove it, split into \(\{|h|\le\delta\}\) and \(\{|h|>\delta\}\). On the first set the remainder is at most
\(\omega(\delta)|h|\), where \(\omega\) is the modulus of continuity of \(\varphi'\). On the second it is at most \(2\|\varphi'\|_\infty|h|\). Since

\[
\mathbb P(|h|>\delta)\le\frac{\|h\|_2^2}{\delta^2},
\]

absolute continuity of \(\int p^2\) makes the second contribution vanish after division by \(\|h\|_2\); then let \(\delta\downarrow0\).

Applying (23) at the inner and outer activations gives

\[
\nabla_UF=q(U)M^*D,
\]

\[
\nabla_MF=D\otimes H,
\]

\[
\nabla_CF=G.
\tag{24}
\]

Therefore, in the original coordinates,

\[
(\dot U,\dot M,\dot C)
=
-\nabla(F-1)^2.
\]

The middle gradient metric is Hilbert–Schmidt. Trace class is the stronger regularity used for stability and convergence.

---

# 6. Every fixed Euler mesh has the claimed width limit

Fix \(\delta>0\) and a finite number \(K\) of Euler steps. Let

\[
M_{k,n}=M_{0,n}+Q_{k,n}.
\]

The Euler recursion is

\[
X_{k+1,n}=X_{k,n}-2\delta e_{k,n}M_{k,n}^\top D_{k,n},
\]

\[
C_{k+1,n}=C_{k,n}-2\delta e_{k,n}G_{k,n},
\]

\[
Q_{k+1,n}
=
Q_{k,n}
-
2\delta e_{k,n}D_{k,n}\otimes_n H_{k,n}.
\tag{25}
\]

Inductively,

\[
Q_{k,n}
=
-2\delta
\sum_{r<k}
e_{r,n}D_{r,n}\otimes_n H_{r,n}.
\tag{26}
\]

Therefore

\[
M_{k,n}H_{k,n}
=
M_{0,n}H_{k,n}
-
2\delta\sum_{r<k}
e_{r,n}D_{r,n}
\langle H_{r,n},H_{k,n}\rangle_n,
\tag{27}
\]

and

\[
M_{k,n}^{\top}D_{k,n}
=
M_{0,n}^{\top}D_{k,n}
-
2\delta\sum_{r<k}
e_{r,n}H_{r,n}
\langle D_{r,n},D_{k,n}\rangle_n.
\tag{28}
\]

Thus every fixed \((\delta,K)\) recursion uses only:

- \(M_{0,n}\) and \(M_{0,n}^{\top}\);
- coordinate maps;
- finitely many empirical moments.

It is therefore a fixed finite Tensor Program. The master theorem and (13) identify its limit with the Euler scheme for (10). This proves pointwise fixed-mesh identification, including all reused-matrix response terms.

---

# 7. Width-uniform mesh removal

For either finite or limiting Euler schemes,

\[
\|C_{k+1}\|_\infty
\le
(1+2a^2\delta)\|C_k\|_\infty+2a\delta.
\]

Hence, for \(k\delta\le T\),

\[
\|C_k\|_\infty
\le
\bar c_T
:=
\left(c_0+\frac1a\right)e^{2a^2T}-\frac1a.
\tag{29}
\]

Let

\[
\bar E_T=1+a\bar c_T,
\]

\[
\bar R_T=2a\bar E_T\bar c_TT.
\]

Then

\[
\|Q_k\|_1\le\bar R_T,\qquad
\|M_k\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le10+\bar R_T.
\tag{30}
\]

Consequently (16)–(17) produce constants \(L_T,A_T\) independent of \(n\).

If \(Y(t)\) is the exact continuous flow and \(Y_k^\delta\) its Euler scheme, the one-step flow truncation satisfies

\[
\|Y(t_{k+1})-Y(t_k)-\delta\mathcal V(Y(t_k))\|
\le
\frac12L_TA_T\delta^2.
\]

Discrete Grönwall gives

\[
\max_{k\delta\le T}
\rho(Y(k\delta),Y_k^\delta)
\le
\frac{A_T}{2}
\bigl(e^{L_TT}-1\bigr)\delta.
\tag{31}
\]

This estimate holds within every finite-width space and in the limiting operator space, with the same constant.

For any finite collection of required action probes, their \(L^2\) flow/Euler difference is therefore \(O_T(\delta)\). At fixed \(\delta\), the master theorem gives their width convergence at the finitely many mesh times. Truncating the series defining \(d_{\mathrm{req}}\), taking \(n\to\infty\), and then \(\delta\downarrow0\), gives

\[
\sup_{t\le T}
d_{\mathrm{req}}(\mathsf S_n^{\mathrm{flow}}(t),\mathsf S(t))
\longrightarrow0
\]

in probability.

No growing-depth Tensor Program result is used.

---

# 8. Exact GD versus finite-width flow

Let

\[
R=-2eM^\top d.
\]

Equation (1) is

\[
u^+=u+\eta q(u)R.
\]

Because \(\Theta\) is cubic,

\[
\Theta(u+\eta q(u)R)
=
\Theta(u)+\eta R
+\eta^2u q(u)^2R^2
+\frac{\eta^3}{3}q(u)^3R^3.
\tag{32}
\]

Thus exact GD is explicit Euler for (10), except for the exact defect

\[
\zeta
=
\eta^2u q(u)^2R^2
+\frac{\eta^3}{3}q(u)^3R^3.
\]

Using (9),

\[
\|\zeta\|_n
\le
\frac{3\sqrt3}{16}
\eta^2\sqrt n\,\|R\|_n^2
+
\frac13\eta^3n\,\|R\|_n^3.
\tag{33}
\]

On the bounded region (29)–(30),

\[
\|R\|_n
\le
2\bar E_T(10+\bar R_T)\bar c_T
=:\bar r_T.
\]

Perturbed Euler Grönwall now yields

\[
\sup_{k\eta\le T}
\rho(Y_{k,n}^{\mathrm{GD}},Y_n^{\mathrm{flow}}(k\eta))
\le
C_T\left(
\eta+\eta\sqrt n+\eta^2n
\right).
\tag{34}
\]

With \(\eta=n^{-2}\),

\[
\eta+\eta\sqrt n+\eta^2n
=
n^{-2}+n^{-3/2}+n^{-3}
=
O(n^{-3/2}).
\tag{35}
\]

The same estimate holds uniformly between nodes after polygonal interpolation because the \(u,M,c\) slopes are uniformly bounded in their normalized norms.

Combining (31), fixed-mesh width convergence, and (35) proves the claimed full diagonal limit.

---

# 9. Uniform integrability and reused-operator terms

The only unbounded dynamically important right field is

\[
P=M^*D.
\]

Let \(P_n^\delta\) be the piecewise-constant coarse-Euler approximation. For every \(R>0\),

\[
\begin{aligned}
\sup_{t\le T}
\left\langle
P_n(t)^2\mathbf 1_{\{|P_n(t)|>R\}}
\right\rangle_n
\le{}&
2\sup_{t\le T}
\left\langle
(P_n^\delta(t))^2
\mathbf 1_{\{|P_n^\delta(t)|>R/2\}}
\right\rangle_n\\
&+
4\sup_{t\le T}\|P_n(t)-P_n^\delta(t)\|_n^2.
\end{aligned}
\tag{36}
\]

For fixed \(\delta\), there are finitely many Tensor Program states. For any \(p>2\),

\[
x^2\mathbf1_{\{|x|>R/2\}}
\le
(2/R)^{p-2}|x|^p,
\]

and the master theorem gives convergence of every fixed \(p\)-th empirical moment. The second term in (36) is \(O_T(\delta^2)\).

Given \(\varepsilon>0\), first choose \(\delta\) so the second term is below \(\varepsilon/2\), then choose \(R\), and finally \(n\). Hence

\[
\lim_{R\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}
\mathbb P\!\left[
\sup_{t\le T}
\left\langle
P_n(t)^2\mathbf1_{\{|P_n(t)|>R\}}
\right\rangle_n>\varepsilon
\right]
=0.
\tag{37}
\]

Joint \(W_2\) convergence of \((U_n,P_n)\), together with (37), gives \(L^2\) convergence of

\[
q(U_n)P_n,\qquad q(U_n)^2P_n
\]

by truncating \(P_n\), applying the bounded-Lipschitz map on the truncation, and then removing the truncation.

Let

\[
p=q(U)^2P.
\]

The reused forward action is handled by

\[
\|M_np_n-M_n^\delta p_n^\delta\|_2
\le
\|M_n-M_n^\delta\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|p_n\|_2
+
\|M_n^\delta\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|p_n-p_n^\delta\|_2.
\tag{38}
\]

The first term follows from the trace-class state estimate, and the second from (37). At fixed \(\delta\), \(M_n^\delta p_n^\delta\) is a finite Tensor Program probe. Thus

\[
M_n(q(U_n)^2P_n)
\]

converges to the exact term in (18).

This closes all kernel and second-layer response terms without an aggregate-adjoint assumption.

---

# 10. Hidden paths and exact-GD velocities

On a GD interval \([k\eta,(k+1)\eta)\), the polygonal parameter interpolation satisfies

\[
\dot u_n=-2e_{k,n}q(u_{k,n})P_{k,n}.
\tag{39}
\]

Differentiating \(s_n(t)=M_n(t)\arctan u_n(t)\) gives exactly

\[
\dot s_n
=
-2e_{k,n}\left[
D_{k,n}
\langle H_{k,n},h_n(t)\rangle_n
+
M_n(t)
\left(
q(u_n(t))q(u_{k,n})P_{k,n}
\right)
\right].
\tag{40}
\]

Inside one step,

\[
\|u_n(t)-u_{k,n}\|_\infty
\le
C_T\eta\sqrt n.
\]

Since \(\eta=n^{-2}\), this tends to zero. Equations (37)–(38), the uniform state convergence, and (39)–(40) imply

\[
\dot u_n\longrightarrow\dot U,
\qquad
\dot s_n\longrightarrow\dot S
\]

in the empirical \(L^2([0,T])\) sense. Therefore

\[
V_n^{(\ell)}(\cdot)^2
\longrightarrow
V^{(\ell)}(\cdot)^2
\quad\text{in }L^1(0,T)
\]

in probability, for \(\ell=1,2\).

For path laws, use the following deterministic estimate. If \(x,y\) are absolutely continuous and \(t_k=k\delta\), then

\[
\frac1n\sum_i
\sup_{t\le T}|x_i(t)-y_i(t)|^2
\le
3\sum_k\|x(t_k)-y(t_k)\|_n^2
+
3\delta\int_0^T
\bigl(\|\dot x(t)\|_n^2+\|\dot y(t)\|_n^2\bigr)\,dt.
\tag{41}
\]

At a fixed coarse mesh, joint \(W_2\) convergence of all mesh values follows from the master theorem. Pushing through piecewise-linear interpolation and using (41) gives

\[
\overline\mu_n^{(\ell)}
\longrightarrow
\overline\mu^{(\ell)}
\quad\text{in probability in }W_2(C([0,T])).
\tag{42}
\]

For exact GD versus the finite flow, the squared knot error from (35) is \(O(n^{-3})\); there are \(O(T/\eta)=O(n^2)\) knots. Thus (41) gives total squared path error

\[
O(n^{-1})+O(\eta)\longrightarrow0.
\]

This proves path tightness, identification, and path-square uniform integrability.

---

# 11. Nondegeneracy and feature learning

Let

\[
U_0\sim N(0,1),\qquad
H_0=\arctan U_0,
\qquad
m=\mathbb E H_0^2>0.
\]

The master law for the first multiplication gives

\[
Z:=\Gamma H_0\sim N(0,m).
\]

Define

\[
G_0=\arctan Z,
\qquad
Y=G_0q(Z),
\]

\[
\nu=\mathbb E Y^2>0,
\qquad
\Xi=\Gamma^*Y,
\]

\[
d_0=\mathbb E[q(U_0)^2\Xi^2].
\]

By the adjoint identity,

\[
\langle H_0,\Xi\rangle_R
=
\langle Z,Y\rangle_L
=
\mathbb E\!\left[
\frac{Z\arctan Z}{1+Z^2}
\right]
>0.
\]

Therefore \(\Xi\ne0\), and since \(q(U_0)>0\) almost surely,

\[
d_0>0.
\tag{43}
\]

Let

\[
\mathcal R
=
mI+\Gamma M_{q(U_0)^2}\Gamma^*.
\]

Then

\[
\langle Y,\mathcal RY\rangle
=
m\nu+d_0>0.
\tag{44}
\]

At \(t=0\),

\[
C_0=0,\qquad e_0=-1,\qquad M_0=\Gamma.
\]

Strong continuity of the ODE gives

\[
C(t)=2tG_0+o_{L^2}(t),
\]

\[
D(t)=2tY+o_{L^2}(t),
\]

\[
X(t)-X_0
=
2t^2\Xi+o_{L^2}(t^2),
\]

\[
M(t)-\Gamma
=
2t^2Y\otimes H_0+o_{\mathfrak S_1}(t^2).
\tag{45}
\]

The directional Nemytskii lemma following from (23) yields

\[
U(t)-U_0
=
2t^2q(U_0)\Xi+o_{L^2}(t^2),
\tag{46}
\]

and hence

\[
S(t)-Z
=
2t^2\mathcal RY+o_{L^2}(t^2).
\tag{47}
\]

Consequently,

\[
K^{(1)}(t)=4d_0t^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
K^{(2)}(t)=4m\nu t^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
K^{(3)}(t)
=
k_3+4(d_0+m\nu)t^2+o(t^2),
\tag{48}
\]

where

\[
k_3=\mathbb E(\arctan Z)^2>0.
\]

Thus

\[
K(t)
=
k_3+8(d_0+m\nu)t^2+o(t^2).
\tag{49}
\]

The hidden velocities satisfy

\[
V^{(1)}(t)^2
=
16d_0t^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
V^{(2)}(t)^2
=
16\|\mathcal RY\|_2^2t^2+o(t^2).
\tag{50}
\]

Their squared displacements satisfy

\[
\mathbb E|U(t)-U_0|^2
=
4d_0t^4+o(t^4),
\]

\[
\mathbb E|S(t)-Z|^2
=
4\|\mathcal RY\|_2^2t^4+o(t^4).
\tag{51}
\]

All leading coefficients are strictly positive.

---

# 12. Surviving nonlinearity and loss movement

For a square-integrable random variable \(Z_*\) with positive variance,

\[
\inf_{a,b}
\mathbb E|\arctan Z_*-aZ_*-b|^2
=
\operatorname{Var}(\arctan Z_*)
-
\frac{\operatorname{Cov}(Z_*,\arctan Z_*)^2}
{\operatorname{Var}(Z_*)}.
\tag{52}
\]

For either nondegenerate initial Gaussian \(U_0\) or \(Z\), the right-hand side is positive. Equality would imply that \(\arctan z\) agrees with an affine function Gaussian-almost surely, hence everywhere by continuity and full support, which is impossible.

The quantities in (52) depend continuously on the hidden marginal in \(W_2\), provided the variance remains bounded away from zero. Thus both nonlinear residuals remain strictly positive for all sufficiently small \(t\).

At initialization,

\[
F(0)=0,\qquad e(0)=-1.
\]

From (19),

\[
F'(0)=2k_3>0,
\]

and therefore

\[
L'(0)=2e(0)F'(0)=-4k_3<0.
\tag{53}
\]

Choose \(T_*>0\) sufficiently small that, for every \(0<t<T_*\):

- both hidden variances remain positive;
- both regression residuals remain positive;
- \(K^{(1)}(t)>0\) and \(K^{(2)}(t)>0\);
- \(K(t)>K(0)\);
- both hidden displacement quantities are positive;
- \(L(t)<L(0)\).

This is possible by (43)–(53).

It follows that for every \(0<T<T_*\),

\[
0<
\int_0^T K^{(\ell)}(t)\,dt
<\infty,
\qquad \ell=1,2,3,
\]

\[
0<
\int_0^T V^{(\ell)}(t)^2\,dt
<\infty,
\qquad \ell=1,2,
\]

\[
\int_0^T\mathcal N^{(\ell)}(t)\,dt>0,
\qquad \ell=1,2,
\]

and all remaining requirements follow from the convergence proved above.

---

## Audit result

Six isolated audit tracks were run twice, covering:

1. exact scaling and every \(n\)-factor;
2. the exact scope of the Tensor Program theorem;
3. projective consistency and construction of the two probability algebras;
4. reused \(M,M^\top\) action and adjointness;
5. Banach well-posedness, the \(L^\infty\) envelope, and global continuation;
6. Hilbert-gradient differentiability;
7. exact-GD/Euler defects and the diagonal \(n^{-2}\) step;
8. uniform integrability and the term \(M(q(U)^2P)\);
9. exact polygonal second-layer velocities;
10. \(W_2(C([0,T]))\) path convergence;
11. every positivity and quantifier in properties 1–7.

The audits found and forced four substantive repairs:

- replacing the false bare-Hilbert/GNS construction by genuine probability algebras;
- replacing the arbitrary-polynomial topology by \(d_{\mathrm{req}}\);
- separating the Hilbert–Schmidt gradient metric from trace-class control;
- removing the auxiliary readout seed and historical rank-one factor list from the restart state.

After those repairs, no unclosed limit exchange, growing-depth Tensor Program invocation, reused-adjoint assumption, or trajectory-dependent closure remains.

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 22683 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.298Z | assistant | final_answer ===
No—not yet as a fully rigorous continuous-time theorem. The algebra, fixed-mesh width limit, formal operator flow, and all nondegeneracy coefficients extend to three hidden layers. But the continuous-time mesh-removal argument encounters a genuine new unbounded-multiplier obstruction. Three independent adversarial audits found the same gap.

I interpret \(L=3\) as three hidden layers and four parameter blocks.

## 1. Exact scaling and finite-width equations

Take

\[
\phi(x)=\arctan x,\qquad q(x)=\phi'(x)=\frac1{1+x^2},
\]

with

\[
\operatorname{Var}(W^{(1)})=1,\qquad
\operatorname{Var}(W^{(2)}_{ji})
=
\operatorname{Var}(W^{(3)}_{kj})
=\frac1n,
\]

\[
\operatorname{Var}(W^{(4)}_k)=\frac1{n^4}.
\]

Use

\[
\lambda_1=n,\qquad
\lambda_2=\lambda_3=1,\qquad
\lambda_4=\frac1n,
\qquad
\eta_{0,n}=n^{-2}.
\]

Then

\[
\eta_{0,n}\lambda_1=n^{-1},\quad
\eta_{0,n}\lambda_2=\eta_{0,n}\lambda_3=n^{-2},\quad
\eta_{0,n}\lambda_4=n^{-3}.
\]

Define

\[
U=W^{(1)},\qquad
M_2=W^{(2)},\qquad
M_3=W^{(3)},\qquad
C=nW^{(4)},
\]

and

\[
H_1=\phi(U),\qquad
S_2=M_2H_1,\qquad
H_2=\phi(S_2),
\]

\[
S_3=M_3H_2,\qquad
H_3=\phi(S_3),
\]

\[
F_n=\langle C,H_3\rangle_n,\qquad e=F_n-1.
\]

The backpropagated fields are

\[
D_3=Cq(S_3),
\]

\[
B_2=M_3^\top D_3,
\qquad
D_2=q(S_2)B_2,
\]

\[
P_1=M_2^\top D_2.
\]

Direct differentiation gives

\[
\nabla_UF_n=\frac1nq(U)P_1,
\]

\[
\nabla_{M_2}F_n=\frac1nD_2H_1^\top,
\qquad
\nabla_{M_3}F_n=\frac1nD_3H_2^\top,
\]

\[
\nabla_{W^{(4)}}F_n=H_3.
\]

Thus exact GD is

\[
U^+=U-2\eta e\,q(U)P_1,
\tag{1}
\]

\[
M_2^+=M_2-2\eta e\,D_2\otimes_nH_1,
\tag{2}
\]

\[
M_3^+=M_3-2\eta e\,D_3\otimes_nH_2,
\tag{3}
\]

\[
C^+=C-2\eta e\,H_3.
\tag{4}
\]

The four exact kernel blocks are

\[
K_n^{(1)}=\|q(U)P_1\|_n^2,
\]

\[
K_n^{(2)}=\|H_1\|_n^2\|D_2\|_n^2,
\]

\[
K_n^{(3)}=\|H_2\|_n^2\|D_3\|_n^2,
\]

\[
K_n^{(4)}=\|H_3\|_n^2.
\tag{5}
\]

All width factors therefore balance correctly.

## 2. The fixed-mesh width limit is rigorous

Write

\[
M_{r,k}=\Gamma_r+Q_{r,k},\qquad r=2,3,
\]

where \(\Gamma_2,\Gamma_3\) are the two independent initialization matrices. At a fixed Euler mesh \(\delta\),

\[
Q_{2,k}
=
-2\delta\sum_{j<k}
e_jD_{2,j}\otimes H_{1,j},
\]

\[
Q_{3,k}
=
-2\delta\sum_{j<k}
e_jD_{3,j}\otimes H_{2,j}.
\tag{6}
\]

Hence, for example,

\[
M_{3,k}^\top D_{3,k}
=
\Gamma_3^\top D_{3,k}
-
2\delta\sum_{j<k}
e_jH_{2,j}
\langle D_{3,j},D_{3,k}\rangle,
\tag{7}
\]

with analogous identities for every forward and adjoint multiplication.

For every fixed number of steps, the computation therefore contains only:

- the two independent Gaussian matrices and their transposes;
- coordinatewise pseudo-Lipschitz maps;
- finitely many empirical moments.

Consequently, the fixed-\((\delta,K)\) recursion is a finite Tensor Program. Setup G.2, Assumption L.4, and Theorem G.4 of the [Tensor Programs IV supplement](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/yang21c/yang21c-supp.pdf) apply exactly. They allow finitely many independent matrices, arbitrary finite reuse of each transpose, and singular limiting Gram matrices.

The same countable-prefix/Kolmogorov argument used at \(L=2\) produces three probability algebras

\[
\mathcal H_r=L^2(\Omega_r),\qquad r=1,2,3,
\]

and bounded operators

\[
\Gamma_2:\mathcal H_1\to\mathcal H_2,
\qquad
\Gamma_3:\mathcal H_2\to\mathcal H_3
\]

with their genuine Hilbert adjoints. Thus the fixed-mesh three-sort operator DAG is unconditional.

## 3. Formal limiting gradient flow

Let

\[
\Theta(u)=u+\frac{u^3}{3},\qquad X=\Theta(U).
\]

The formal operator flow is

\[
\dot X=-2eM_2^*D_2,
\tag{8}
\]

\[
\dot M_2=-2eD_2\otimes H_1,
\tag{9}
\]

\[
\dot M_3=-2eD_3\otimes H_2,
\tag{10}
\]

\[
\dot C=-2eH_3.
\tag{11}
\]

In the original \(U\)-coordinate it is formally the Hilbert gradient flow of

\[
\mathcal L=(F-1)^2
\]

on

\[
\mathcal H_1
\times(\Gamma_2+\mathfrak S_2)
\times(\Gamma_3+\mathfrak S_2)
\times\mathcal H_3.
\]

The gradients are

\[
\nabla_UF=q(U)M_2^*D_2,
\]

\[
\nabla_{M_2}F=D_2\otimes H_1,
\qquad
\nabla_{M_3}F=D_3\otimes H_2,
\]

\[
\nabla_CF=H_3.
\]

For every sufficiently regular solution,

\[
\dot F=-2eK,
\]

where

\[
K=
\|q(U)M_2^*D_2\|_2^2
+\|H_1\|_2^2\|D_2\|_2^2
+\|H_2\|_2^2\|D_3\|_2^2
+\|H_3\|_2^2.
\tag{12}
\]

Thus \(|e(t)|\le |e(0)|\), and the bounded activation supplies finite-time a priori bounds for \(C,M_2,M_3,X\). These bounds exclude blowup if a strong solution exists, but do not prove uniqueness or mesh stability.

## 4. The new obstruction

At \(L=2\), the only nonlinear backprop product was

\[
D=Cq(S),
\]

and \(C\) remained in \(L^\infty\). Therefore

\[
\|Cq(S)-\widetilde Cq(\widetilde S)\|_2
\]

was locally Lipschitz in the \(L^2/\mathfrak S_1\) state norm.

At \(L=3\),

\[
D_2=q(S_2)B_2,
\qquad
B_2=M_3^*D_3.
\tag{13}
\]

Here \(B_2\in L^2\) but generally \(B_2\notin L^\infty\). For two states,

\[
D_2-\widetilde D_2
=
q(S_2)(B_2-\widetilde B_2)
+
\bigl(q(S_2)-q(\widetilde S_2)\bigr)\widetilde B_2.
\tag{14}
\]

The last term cannot be bounded by a constant times

\[
\|S_2-\widetilde S_2\|_2.
\]

This failure is genuine.

Consider the nearby state

\[
M_2=\Gamma_2,\qquad M_3=\Gamma_3,\qquad
C=\varepsilon H_3.
\]

Then

\[
D_3=\varepsilon H_3q(S_3)
\]

and

\[
B_2=\varepsilon\Gamma_3^*
\bigl(H_3q(S_3)\bigr).
\]

The transpose master law has the form

\[
\Gamma_3^*Y
=
\sqrt{\mathbb E Y^2}\,G+\kappa H_2,
\tag{15}
\]

where \(G\) is a nondegenerate Gaussian innovation independent of the lower forward fields. Therefore \(B_2\) is unbounded.

Perturb \(Q_2\) by

\[
\Delta Q_2
=
\frac{h\otimes H_1}{\|H_1\|_2^2}.
\]

Then

\[
\Delta Q_2H_1=h,
\qquad
\|\Delta Q_2\|_{\mathfrak S_1}
=
\frac{\|h\|_2}{\|H_1\|_2}.
\tag{16}
\]

The directional variation of \(D_2\) is

\[
\begin{aligned}
\delta D_2
={}&q'(S_2)B_2h\\
&+
q(S_2)M_3^*
\left[
Cq'(S_3)M_3(q(S_2)h)
\right].
\end{aligned}
\tag{17}
\]

The second line defines a bounded \(L^2\to L^2\) operator, since \(C,q,q'\) are bounded and \(M_3\) is bounded.

The first line is multiplication by

\[
q'(S_2)B_2.
\]

For arctangent,

\[
q'(s)=-\frac{2s}{(1+s^2)^2}.
\]

On any interval separated from zero, \(|q'|\) is bounded below. Since \(B_2\) has a nondegenerate conditional Gaussian component,

\[
q'(S_2)B_2\notin L^\infty.
\]

Multiplication by this field is therefore an unbounded operator on \(L^2\). More explicitly, choose sets

\[
E_m\subset
\{|B_2|\ge m,\ |q'(S_2)|\ge c\}
\]

of positive measure and let \(h_m=\varepsilon_m1_{E_m}\), with \(\varepsilon_m\downarrow0\). Taylor’s formula gives

\[
\frac{
\|(q(S_2+h_m)-q(S_2))B_2\|_2
}{
\|h_m\|_2
}
\ge \frac c2m.
\tag{18}
\]

Thus the gradient vector field is not locally Lipschitz in

\[
L^2
\oplus\mathfrak S_1
\oplus\mathfrak S_1
\oplus L^2.
\tag{19}
\]

Consequently, the \(L=2\) dimension-free estimate

\[
\sup_{t\le T}
\|Y_n(t)-Y_n^\delta(t)\|
\le C_T\delta
\]

cannot be reused.

Uniform integrability only proves that \(q(S_{2,n})B_{2,n}\) converges along a sequence already known to converge. It supplies neither uniqueness nor stability of two nearby solutions.

## 5. The exact-GD defect still works algebraically

Set

\[
R=-2eM_2^\top D_2.
\]

Then

\[
U^+=U+\eta q(U)R,
\]

and the exact cubic identity remains

\[
X^+
=
X+\eta R
+
\eta^2Uq(U)^2R^2
+
\frac{\eta^3}{3}q(U)^3R^3.
\]

Thus

\[
\|\zeta\|_n
\le
\frac{3\sqrt3}{16}
\eta^2\sqrt n\,\|R\|_n^2
+
\frac13\eta^3n\,\|R\|_n^3.
\tag{20}
\]

If a width-uniform stability theorem existed, \(\eta=n^{-2}\) would again make the accumulated defect vanish. Equation (20), however, does not supply the missing stability theorem.

## 6. Conditional small-time coefficients

Although the full flow theorem is open, its local coefficients can be calculated rigorously conditional on a sufficiently regular strong solution.

Let

\[
A=U_0,\qquad h_1=\arctan A,\qquad
m_1=\|h_1\|_2^2,
\]

\[
Z_2=\Gamma_2h_1,\qquad
h_2=\arctan Z_2,\qquad
m_2=\|h_2\|_2^2,
\]

\[
Z_3=\Gamma_3h_2,\qquad
h_3=\arctan Z_3,\qquad
m_3=\|h_3\|_2^2.
\]

Define

\[
y_3=h_3q(Z_3),\qquad
\nu_3=\|y_3\|_2^2,
\]

\[
b_2=\Gamma_3^*y_3,\qquad
y_2=q(Z_2)b_2,\qquad
\nu_2=\|y_2\|_2^2,
\]

\[
p_1=\Gamma_2^*y_2,\qquad
d_1=\|q(A)p_1\|_2^2.
\]

The bottom coefficient is strictly positive. Put

\[
v=Z_2q(Z_2).
\]

Because \(\Gamma_3\) is independent of the \(\Gamma_2\)-generated pair \((v,h_2)\), Gaussian regression gives

\[
\langle v,b_2\rangle
=
\frac{\langle v,h_2\rangle}{m_2}
\mathbb E\!\left[
Z_3\arctan(Z_3)q(Z_3)
\right]
>0.
\tag{21}
\]

Both factors are positive since

\[
z\arctan(z)q(z)>0
\quad(z\ne0).
\]

Therefore

\[
\langle h_1,p_1\rangle
=
\langle Z_2,y_2\rangle
=
\langle v,b_2\rangle>0.
\]

Hence \(p_1\ne0\), and \(q(A)>0\) almost surely implies

\[
d_1>0.
\tag{22}
\]

Define

\[
\mathcal R_2
=
m_1I+
\Gamma_2M_{q(A)^2}\Gamma_2^*,
\]

\[
\mathcal R_3
=
m_2I+
\Gamma_3M_{q(Z_2)}
\mathcal R_2
M_{q(Z_2)}\Gamma_3^*.
\]

Then

\[
\langle y_2,\mathcal R_2y_2\rangle
=
m_1\nu_2+d_1,
\]

\[
\langle y_3,\mathcal R_3y_3\rangle
=
m_2\nu_3+m_1\nu_2+d_1.
\tag{23}
\]

The strong expansions would be

\[
U(t)
=
A+2t^2q(A)p_1+o_{L^2}(t^2),
\]

\[
S_2(t)
=
Z_2+2t^2\mathcal R_2y_2+o_{L^2}(t^2),
\]

\[
S_3(t)
=
Z_3+2t^2\mathcal R_3y_3+o_{L^2}(t^2).
\tag{24}
\]

Consequently,

\[
K^{(1)}(t)=4d_1t^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
K^{(2)}(t)=4m_1\nu_2t^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
K^{(3)}(t)=4m_2\nu_3t^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
K^{(4)}(t)
=
m_3+
4(d_1+m_1\nu_2+m_2\nu_3)t^2
+o(t^2),
\]

and therefore

\[
K(t)
=
m_3+
8(d_1+m_1\nu_2+m_2\nu_3)t^2
+o(t^2).
\tag{25}
\]

The three feature speeds are

\[
V_1(t)^2=16d_1t^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
V_2(t)^2
=
16\|\mathcal R_2y_2\|_2^2t^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
V_3(t)^2
=
16\|\mathcal R_3y_3\|_2^2t^2+o(t^2).
\tag{26}
\]

All coefficients are strictly positive. Also,

\[
F(0)=0,\qquad L'(0)=-4m_3<0.
\]

Thus nondegeneracy is not the obstruction.

## Audited conclusion

The following parts extend rigorously:

- exact finite-width scaling and GD equations;
- all four kernel normalizations;
- every fixed-mesh width limit;
- the three-population Gaussian action and both reused adjoints;
- the formal operator gradient equations;
- the energy identity and finite-time a priori bounds;
- the exact transformed-GD defect;
- strict positivity of all conditional small-time feature and kernel coefficients.

The requested full theorem does not currently extend because the following remain unproved:

- existence and uniqueness of a restartable strong \(L=3\) operator flow;
- a width-independent Euler/flow stability estimate;
- uniform mesh removal;
- the joint exact-GD/width limit;
- resulting Wasserstein path convergence.

A valid positive extension requires a new weighted, Orlicz, Gaussian-Sobolev, or reachable-source norm controlling the unbounded multiplier \(M_3^*D_3\). The \(L=2\) proof does not contain such a theorem, and fixed-step Tensor Programs do not provide it.

So the rigorous answer is: the \(L=3\) fixed-mesh and local theory is established, but the full continuous-time result remains open.

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 22839 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.303Z | assistant | final_answer ===
No—not with “arbitrary configuration” and all the properties of the one-sample theorem.

There are two separate conclusions:

1. The active-feature-learning theorem is false for arbitrary datasets; explicit counterexamples exist.
2. For a general fixed finite dataset, the fixed-mesh width limit is rigorous, but the continuous-time mesh-removal proof develops a new unbounded-multiplier gap. The previous \(L^2/\mathfrak S_1\) proof closes only for a special input geometry described below.

I interpret the dataset size \(m\) and input dimension \(d\) as fixed while \(n\to\infty\).

## 1. Exact finite-dataset dynamics

Let the data be

\[
(x_a,y_a)\in\mathbb R^d\times\mathbb R,
\qquad a=1,\dots,m,
\]

and use the averaged loss

\[
L_n=\frac1m\sum_{a=1}^m(f_n^a-y_a)^2.
\]

For the same two-hidden-layer network, define

\[
U_i^a=w_i\cdot x_a,\qquad
H_i^a=\arctan U_i^a,
\]

\[
S_j^a=\sum_iM_{ji}H_i^a,\qquad
G_j^a=\arctan S_j^a,
\]

\[
C=nW^{(3)},\qquad
f_n^a=\langle C,G^a\rangle_n,
\qquad
e_a=f_n^a-y_a.
\]

Set

\[
q(z)=\frac1{1+z^2},
\qquad
D^a=Cq(S^a),
\qquad
P^a=M^\top D^a,
\qquad
A^a=q(U^a)P^a.
\]

Use the same scaling

\[
\operatorname{Var}(w_{ir})=1,\qquad
\operatorname{Var}(M_{ji})=\frac1n,\qquad
\operatorname{Var}(W_j^{(3)})=\frac1{n^4},
\]

\[
\lambda_1=n,\qquad
\lambda_2=1,\qquad
\lambda_3=\frac1n,
\qquad
\eta_{0,n}=n^{-2}.
\]

Direct differentiation gives

\[
\nabla_{W^{(1)}}f_n^a
=
\frac1n A^a x_a^\top,
\]

\[
\nabla_M f_n^a
=
\frac1nD^a(H^a)^\top,
\]

\[
\nabla_{W^{(3)}}f_n^a=G^a.
\]

Hence exact full-batch GD is

\[
W^{(1)+}
=
W^{(1)}
-\frac{2\eta}{m}\sum_a e_aA^ax_a^\top,
\tag{1}
\]

\[
M^+
=
M-\frac{2\eta}{m}
\sum_a e_aD^a\otimes_nH^a,
\tag{2}
\]

\[
C^+
=
C-\frac{2\eta}{m}\sum_a e_aG^a.
\tag{3}
\]

All normalization factors are exact.

## 2. Kernel matrix and loss identity

The scalar kernel becomes an \(m\times m\) positive-semidefinite matrix. Its blocks are

\[
K_{ab}^{(1)}
=
(x_a\cdot x_b)
\langle A^a,A^b\rangle_n,
\tag{4}
\]

\[
K_{ab}^{(2)}
=
\langle D^a,D^b\rangle_n
\langle H^a,H^b\rangle_n,
\tag{5}
\]

\[
K_{ab}^{(3)}
=
\langle G^a,G^b\rangle_n.
\tag{6}
\]

Each is a Gram matrix of the corresponding parameter gradients.

Along continuous finite-width gradient flow,

\[
\dot f_a
=
-\frac2m\sum_bK_{ab}e_b,
\]

and therefore

\[
\dot L_n
=
-\frac4{m^2}e^\top K e
\le0.
\tag{7}
\]

Thus strict loss decrease is equivalent to

\[
e(t)\notin\ker K(t)
\]

on a set of times of positive measure. Merely having \(\operatorname{tr}K^{(\ell)}>0\) does not imply that block \(\ell\) participates in training.

## 3. Why the universal active theorem is false

Take two identical nonzero inputs with conflicting labels:

\[
x_1=x_2=x\ne0,
\qquad
y_1=1,\quad y_2=-1.
\]

The network necessarily produces the same prediction on both samples:

\[
f_1=f_2=f.
\]

Consequently,

\[
L
=
\frac12\bigl((f-1)^2+(f+1)^2\bigr)
=
f^2+1.
\]

Under the proposed scaling, the limiting readout initialization is \(C_0=0\), hence \(f(0)=0\). Therefore

\[
\nabla L(0)=2f(0)\nabla f(0)=0.
\]

The limiting dynamics are stationary:

\[
C(t)=0,\qquad
M(t)=M(0),\qquad
W^{(1)}(t)=W^{(1)}(0).
\]

This example has nonzero inputs and nondegenerate nonlinear hidden laws, but:

- neither hidden layer moves;
- \(K^{(1)}\) and \(K^{(2)}\) do not contribute to training;
- the loss never decreases;
- there is no feature learning.

An even simpler counterexample is \(x=0\): in a bias-free network, the first hidden preactivation is identically zero for every parameter value, so its variance and velocity can never be positive.

Thus the previous seven properties cannot hold uniformly over arbitrary input-label configurations.

## 4. What does extend unconditionally: every fixed Euler mesh

At initialization,

\[
(U_0^1,\dots,U_0^m)
\]

is a jointly Gaussian coordinate mark with covariance

\[
\mathbb E[U_0^aU_0^b]
=
x_a\cdot x_b.
\]

For a fixed Euler mesh \(\delta\) and a fixed number of steps \(K\), write

\[
M_{k,n}=M_{0,n}+Q_{k,n}.
\]

From (2),

\[
Q_{k,n}
=
-\frac{2\delta}{m}
\sum_{r<k}\sum_a
e_{r,a}D_{r,n}^a\otimes_nH_{r,n}^a.
\tag{8}
\]

Hence

\[
\begin{aligned}
M_{k,n}H_{k,n}^b
={}&M_{0,n}H_{k,n}^b\\
&-\frac{2\delta}{m}
\sum_{r<k,a}
e_{r,a}D_{r,n}^a
\langle H_{r,n}^a,H_{k,n}^b\rangle_n,
\end{aligned}
\tag{9}
\]

and

\[
\begin{aligned}
M_{k,n}^\top D_{k,n}^b
={}&M_{0,n}^\top D_{k,n}^b\\
&-\frac{2\delta}{m}
\sum_{r<k,a}
e_{r,a}H_{r,n}^a
\langle D_{r,n}^a,D_{k,n}^b\rangle_n.
\end{aligned}
\tag{10}
\]

For fixed \((m,\delta,K)\), the complete training recursion therefore uses only:

- finitely many jointly Gaussian initial vector marks;
- \(M_{0,n}\) and \(M_{0,n}^\top\);
- coordinatewise pseudo-Lipschitz maps;
- finitely many empirical moments.

It is a fixed finite Tensor Program. The fixed-program master theorem applies, including when the input Gram matrix is singular; see Setup G.2, Assumption L.4, and Theorem G.4 in the [Tensor Programs IV supplement](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/yang21c/yang21c-supp.pdf).

Therefore, for every fixed mesh:

- all joint sample-feature laws converge;
- every forward and backward field converges jointly;
- all cross-sample Gram quantities converge;
- every output, residual, kernel-matrix entry, and the loss converge;
- the reused Gaussian action and its adjoint are identified exactly.

This part holds for every fixed finite dataset.

## 5. The generic continuous-time obstruction

Let

\[
\mathsf G_{ab}=x_a\cdot x_b
\]

be the input Gram matrix. The formal limiting first-layer equation is

\[
\dot U^b
=
-\frac2m
\sum_a
\mathsf G_{ba}e_aq(U^a)P^a.
\tag{11}
\]

For one sample, the coordinate

\[
X=\Theta(U),\qquad
\Theta'(u)=q(u)^{-1}=1+u^2,
\]

removes the only factor \(q(U)\).

For several samples, applying \(\Theta\) componentwise gives

\[
\dot X^b
=
-\frac2m
\sum_a
\mathsf G_{ba}e_a
\frac{q(U^a)}{q(U^b)}P^a.
\tag{12}
\]

Only diagonal terms simplify. A generic off-diagonal Gram entry leaves the random ratio

\[
\frac{q(U^a)}{q(U^b)}
=
\frac{1+(U^b)^2}{1+(U^a)^2},
\]

which is unbounded.

Working in the original \(W^{(1)}\) coordinate does not repair this. The problematic map is

\[
(U^a,P^a)\longmapsto q(U^a)P^a.
\tag{13}
\]

Here \(P^a=M^*D^a\in L^2\), but it generally has an unbounded Gaussian component. For two nearby fields,

\[
\begin{aligned}
q(U^a)P^a-q(\widetilde U^a)\widetilde P^a
={}&q(U^a)(P^a-\widetilde P^a)\\
&+\bigl(q(U^a)-q(\widetilde U^a)\bigr)\widetilde P^a.
\end{aligned}
\tag{14}
\]

The second term has no bound of the form

\[
C\|U^a-\widetilde U^a\|_2
\]

unless \(\widetilde P^a\in L^\infty\).

Indeed, at a state where \(P^a\) is unbounded and \(q'(U^a)\ne0\) on a positive-measure set, choose

\[
E_R\subseteq
\{|P^a|\ge R,\ |q'(U^a)|\ge c\}
\]

and a sufficiently small perturbation

\[
h_R=\varepsilon_R\mathbf1_{E_R}.
\]

Taylor’s formula gives

\[
\frac{
\|(q(U^a+h_R)-q(U^a))P^a\|_2
}{
\|h_R\|_2
}
\ge\frac c2R.
\]

Thus the local Lipschitz constants diverge.

Consequently, for a generic dataset:

- the earlier Picard uniqueness theorem does not apply;
- the width-uniform \(O(\delta)\) Euler estimate is not available;
- fixed-mesh convergence cannot presently be lifted to continuous time;
- \(\eta_{0,n}=n^{-2}\) does not by itself close the joint diagonal limit.

Uniform integrability identifies (13) along a sequence already known to converge, but it cannot provide uniqueness or the missing stability estimate.

This does not prove that the generic multi-sample limit fails to exist. It proves that the earlier theorem does not extend to it. A stronger weighted, Orlicz, Gaussian-Sobolev, or graph-norm theory would be needed.

## 6. Exact class for which the old proof does extend

The componentwise natural-coordinate cancellation closes precisely when

\[
\mathsf G\operatorname{diag}(q(U))
=
\operatorname{diag}(q(U))\mathsf G
\quad\text{almost surely}.
\tag{15}
\]

Entrywise,

\[
\mathsf G_{ab}\bigl(q(U^a)-q(U^b)\bigr)=0.
\tag{16}
\]

For \(q(u)=(1+u^2)^{-1}\), if \(\mathsf G_{ab}\ne0\), equation (16) requires

\[
(U^a)^2=(U^b)^2
\quad\text{almost surely}.
\]

Since \(U^a=W\cdot x_a\) and the Gaussian \(W\) has full support, this is equivalent to

\[
x_b=\pm x_a.
\]

Therefore the dataset must admit a partition into blocks \(B_r\) such that

\[
x_a=\varepsilon_a v_r,
\qquad
\varepsilon_a\in\{-1,1\},
\qquad a\in B_r,
\tag{17}
\]

and

\[
v_r\cdot v_s=0
\qquad(r\ne s).
\tag{18}
\]

Pairwise orthogonal inputs are the singleton-block case. Unequal collinear magnitudes are not enough.

For (17)–(18), put

\[
V^r=W\cdot v_r,\qquad X^r=\Theta(V^r).
\]

Oddness of \(\arctan\) and evenness of \(q\) give, for \(a\in B_r\),

\[
U^a=\varepsilon_aV^r,\qquad
H^a=\varepsilon_aH^r,
\]

\[
S^a=\varepsilon_aS^r,\qquad
G^a=\varepsilon_aG^r,
\]

\[
D^a=D^r,\qquad P^a=P^r.
\]

Hence

\[
\dot X^r
=
-\frac{2\|v_r\|^2}{m}
P^r
\sum_{a\in B_r}\varepsilon_a e_a.
\tag{19}
\]

The unbounded backward field now appears linearly, with no state-dependent multiplier. The remaining \(M,C\) equations contain only finite sums of the same terms controlled in the one-sample proof.

Thus, under (17)–(18):

- the operator flow is locally Lipschitz in the transformed \(L^2/\mathfrak S_1\) state;
- global a priori bounds hold;
- the width-uniform Euler error is \(O_T(\delta)\);
- fixed-mesh convergence lifts to continuous time;
- the exact-GD defect is again
  \[
  O_T(\eta+\eta\sqrt n+\eta^2n);
  \]
- \(\eta=n^{-2}\) gives the full joint diagonal limit;
- the vector-valued hidden path laws converge in
  \[
  W_2\!\left(C([0,T];\mathbb R^m)\right);
  \]
- the output vector, full kernel matrix, and loss converge uniformly.

So this is a genuine multi-sample extension, but not an arbitrary-configuration one.

## 7. Label nondegeneracy remains necessary

Within a block define the effective label

\[
\bar y_r
=
\frac1{|B_r|}
\sum_{a\in B_r}\varepsilon_a y_a.
\]

Since \(f_a=\varepsilon_af_r\),

\[
\frac1m\sum_{a\in B_r}(f_a-y_a)^2
=
\frac{|B_r|}{m}(f_r-\bar y_r)^2
+
\text{constant}.
\tag{20}
\]

Training depends only on \(\bar y_r\). If every \(\bar y_r=0\), the zero-output limit is stationary.

More generally, strict loss decrease requires

\[
e^\top K e>0,
\]

activity of parameter block \(\ell\) requires

\[
\int_0^T e(t)^\top K^{(\ell)}(t)e(t)\,dt>0,
\]

and per-sample feature learning requires a nonzero limiting displacement for that sample. These cannot be deduced from inputs alone.

## Conclusion

The fully audited statement is:

- For arbitrary fixed finite datasets, the exact scaling, finite-width equations, kernel matrix, energy identity, and every fixed-mesh width limit extend rigorously.
- The complete active-feature-learning theorem is false for arbitrary input-label configurations.
- For generic correlated inputs, the continuous-time width-first limit remains open because the single-sample natural-coordinate stability argument fails.
- The full joint mean-field/gradient-flow theorem does extend under the explicit orthogonal sign-block condition (17)–(18), subject to separate label/residual nondegeneracy for the activity conclusions.

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 22924 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.305Z | assistant | final_answer ===
No universal theorem can hold for every arbitrary input/\(\{\pm1\}\)-label configuration. The conjecture is false as stated.

This does not mean multi-sample limits never exist: they do for substantial classes, including pairwise orthogonal inputs. What fails is the universal quantifier over arbitrary datasets.

## 1. Universal no-go theorem

Consider any bias-free network of the stated form, with any width-independent \(C^2\) non-affine activation, arbitrary Gaussian variances, and arbitrary positive layerwise learning-rate scalers.

Take a dataset with \(m\ge2\),

\[
x_1=\cdots=x_m=0,
\qquad
y_a\in\{-1,1\}.
\]

For every first-layer neuron and every training time,

\[
z_i^{(1),a}(t)=W_i^{(1)}(t)\cdot x_a=0.
\]

Moreover,

\[
\nabla_{W_i^{(1)}}f_n(x_a)
=
\frac{\partial f_n}{\partial z_i^{(1),a}}x_a
=0.
\]

Therefore, identically at every width,

\[
\dot W_i^{(1)}(t)=0,
\]

\[
V_n^{(1)}(t)^2=0,
\]

\[
K_n^{(1)}(t)=0,
\]

and

\[
\frac1n\sum_i
\left|z_i^{(1),a}(t)-z_i^{(1),a}(0)\right|^2=0.
\]

The first hidden preactivation law is the point mass \(\delta_0\), so its variance is zero.

These conclusions are independent of:

- the activation;
- its nonlinear behavior at zero;
- the initialization variances;
- the learning-rate scalers;
- the width/learning-rate relationship;
- the behavior of the second hidden layer.

Thus properties requiring positive first-hidden variance, first-layer feature learning, nonzero displacement, and an active first parameter block cannot hold.

Hence:

\[
\boxed{\text{There is no construction satisfying all previous properties for every arbitrary dataset.}}
\]

This already settles the universal conjecture.

### A nonzero-input obstruction

The failure is not limited to zero inputs. Take

\[
x_1=x_2=x\ne0,\qquad y_1=1,\quad y_2=-1.
\]

Because the inputs are identical,

\[
f_n(x_1)=f_n(x_2)=f_n(x).
\]

The averaged loss is

\[
L_n
=
\frac12\left[(f_n(x)-1)^2+(f_n(x)+1)^2\right]
=
f_n(x)^2+1.
\]

Under any deterministic mean-field initialization limit arising from independent centered output weights, the initial output must be zero: each finite-width initial output is symmetric about zero, so any deterministic probability limit is zero. Therefore

\[
f(0)=0,\qquad \nabla L(0)=2f(0)\nabla f(0)=0.
\]

The limiting gradient flow is stationary. It has no feature motion, no evolving kernel, and no loss decrease. This obstruction applies to every activation, not only odd activations.

---

## 2. Exact finite-dataset equations

Although the universal active theorem is false, the finite-width algebra extends exactly.

Let

\[
\mathcal D=\{(x_a,y_a)\}_{a=1}^m,
\qquad
x_a\in\mathbb R^d,
\qquad y_a\in\{-1,1\},
\]

with fixed \(m,d\), and use

\[
L_n=\frac1m\sum_{a=1}^m(f_n^a-y_a)^2.
\]

For the two-hidden-layer arctangent network, define

\[
U_i^a=w_i\cdot x_a,\qquad
H_i^a=\arctan U_i^a,
\]

\[
S_j^a=\sum_iM_{ji}H_i^a,\qquad
G_j^a=\arctan S_j^a,
\]

\[
C=nW^{(3)},\qquad
f_n^a=\langle C,G^a\rangle_n,\qquad
e_a=f_n^a-y_a.
\]

Let

\[
q(z)=\frac1{1+z^2},
\qquad
D^a=Cq(S^a),
\qquad
P^a=M^\top D^a,
\qquad
A^a=q(U^a)P^a.
\]

Use the same scaling as in the single-sample theorem:

\[
\operatorname{Var}(w_{ir})=1,
\qquad
\operatorname{Var}(M_{ji})=\frac1n,
\qquad
\operatorname{Var}(W_j^{(3)})=\frac1{n^4},
\]

\[
\lambda_1=n,\qquad
\lambda_2=1,\qquad
\lambda_3=\frac1n,
\qquad
\eta_n=n^{-2}.
\]

Direct differentiation gives the exact GD recurrence

\[
W^{(1)+}
=
W^{(1)}
-\frac{2\eta_n}{m}
\sum_{a=1}^m e_a A^a x_a^\top,
\tag{1}
\]

\[
M^+
=
M-\frac{2\eta_n}{m}
\sum_{a=1}^m e_aD^a\otimes_n H^a,
\tag{2}
\]

\[
C^+
=
C-\frac{2\eta_n}{m}
\sum_{a=1}^m e_aG^a.
\tag{3}
\]

The samplewise kernel is now an \(m\times m\) matrix. Its blocks are

\[
K_{ab}^{(1)}
=
(x_a\cdot x_b)
\langle A^a,A^b\rangle_n,
\tag{4}
\]

\[
K_{ab}^{(2)}
=
\langle D^a,D^b\rangle_n
\langle H^a,H^b\rangle_n,
\tag{5}
\]

\[
K_{ab}^{(3)}
=
\langle G^a,G^b\rangle_n.
\tag{6}
\]

Every block is positive semidefinite because it is a Gram matrix of parameter gradients. Along finite-width gradient flow,

\[
\dot f_a=-\frac2m\sum_bK_{ab}e_b,
\]

and

\[
\dot L_n
=
-\frac4{m^2}e^\top Ke
\le0.
\tag{7}
\]

For multiple samples, block activity must therefore be measured by

\[
\int_0^T e(t)^\top K^{(\ell)}(t)e(t)\,dt>0,
\]

not merely by \(\operatorname{tr}K^{(\ell)}>0\).

---

## 3. What remains rigorously valid for every fixed dataset

For every fixed dataset and every fixed Euler mesh, the full width limit exists.

At initialization,

\[
(U_0^1,\ldots,U_0^m)
\]

is jointly Gaussian with covariance

\[
\operatorname{Cov}(U_0^a,U_0^b)=x_a\cdot x_b.
\]

For a fixed number of steps, the learned middle matrix has the exact representation

\[
Q_{k,n}
=
-\frac{2\delta}{m}
\sum_{r<k}\sum_{a=1}^m
e_{r,a}D_{r,n}^a\otimes_nH_{r,n}^a.
\]

Thus every multiplication by \(M_{k,n}=M_{0,n}+Q_{k,n}\), or its transpose, reduces to:

- multiplication by \(M_{0,n}\) or \(M_{0,n}^\top\);
- finitely many coordinate maps;
- finitely many cross-sample empirical inner products.

For fixed \(m,\delta,k\), this is one finite Tensor Program. The fixed-program master theorem therefore gives joint convergence of all sample fields, outputs, residuals, cross-sample kernels, and moments. Its hypotheses allow a singular initial input Gram matrix and arbitrary reuse of the shared Gaussian matrix and its transpose; see [Yang–Hu, Setup G.2 and Theorem G.4](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/yang21c/yang21c-supp.pdf).

So the following statement is unconditional:

\[
\boxed{\text{Every fixed-mesh, finite-time multi-sample OMFP DAG is rigorously identified.}}
\]

The corresponding hidden empirical states are vector-valued laws on

\[
C([0,T];\mathbb R^m).
\]

---

## 4. Why the continuous-time proof does not extend to generic inputs

The formal continuum first-layer equation is

\[
\dot W
=
-\frac2m\sum_a
e_a q(U^a)P^a x_a,
\qquad U^a=W\cdot x_a.
\tag{8}
\]

Let

\[
\mathsf G_{ab}=x_a\cdot x_b
\]

be the input Gram matrix. Then

\[
\dot U^a
=
-\frac2m
\sum_b
\mathsf G_{ab}e_bq(U^b)P^b.
\tag{9}
\]

For one sample, the coordinate

\[
\Theta(u)=u+\frac{u^3}{3},
\qquad \Theta'(u)=q(u)^{-1},
\]

removes the single factor \(q(U)\).

Applying it componentwise in (9) gives

\[
\frac d{dt}\Theta(U^a)
=
-\frac2m
\sum_b
\mathsf G_{ab}e_b
\frac{q(U^b)}{q(U^a)}P^b.
\tag{10}
\]

For a generic correlated dataset, the ratios in (10) are nonconstant and unbounded.

Tracking \(U\) directly does not help. The problematic map is

\[
(U^a,P^a)\longmapsto q(U^a)P^a.
\]

Although \(P^a\in L^2\), it has a Gaussian, hence generally unbounded, component. For two states,

\[
q(U^a)P^a-q(\widetilde U^a)\widetilde P^a
=
q(U^a)(P^a-\widetilde P^a)
+
\bigl(q(U^a)-q(\widetilde U^a)\bigr)\widetilde P^a.
\]

The last term cannot be controlled by the \(L^2\)-distance of \(U^a,\widetilde U^a\). Infinitesimally it contains multiplication by

\[
q'(U^a)\widetilde P^a,
\]

which is not a bounded \(L^2\to L^2\) operator when that multiplier is unbounded.

Hence, for generic correlated inputs:

- the limiting vector field is not locally Lipschitz in the previous \(L^2/\mathfrak S_1\) state norm;
- the prior Picard uniqueness proof fails;
- the width-uniform \(O(\delta)\) Euler estimate fails;
- fixed-mesh convergence cannot currently be lifted to a restartable continuous-time theorem by the previous argument.

Uniform integrability identifies the product after convergence has already been obtained; it does not prove uniqueness or Euler stability.

Thus an affirmative theorem for every distinct nonzero generic dataset remains open and would require a stronger weighted, Orlicz, or Gaussian-Sobolev action norm.

---

## 5. A genuine positive multi-sample class

The failure is not “multiple samples are impossible.” A substantial class is rigorously covered.

Suppose the inputs admit a partition into blocks \(B_r\) such that

\[
x_a=\varepsilon_a v_r,\qquad
\varepsilon_a\in\{-1,1\},
\qquad a\in B_r,
\]

and

\[
v_r\cdot v_s=0
\qquad(r\ne s).
\tag{11}
\]

This includes arbitrary finite collections of pairwise orthogonal nonzero inputs.

For \(a\in B_r\),

\[
U^a=\varepsilon_aV^r,
\qquad
V^r=W\cdot v_r.
\]

Since \(q\) is even,

\[
q(U^a)=q(V^r).
\]

Orthogonality gives

\[
\dot V^r
=
-\frac{2\|v_r\|^2}{m}
q(V^r)
\sum_{a\in B_r}\varepsilon_a e_aP^a.
\]

Therefore

\[
X^r=\Theta(V^r)
\]

satisfies

\[
\dot X^r
=
-\frac{2\|v_r\|^2}{m}
\sum_{a\in B_r}\varepsilon_a e_aP^a.
\tag{12}
\]

All unbounded backward fields now appear linearly; the troublesome state-dependent multiplier has disappeared. The complete single-sample \(L^2/\mathfrak S_1\) proof extends with only finite \(m\)-dependent constants:

- the operator ODE is well-posed and restartable;
- finite-width and limiting Euler errors are \(O_T(\delta)\);
- exact GD with \(\eta_n=n^{-2}\) converges jointly to the operator gradient flow;
- vector-valued hidden path laws converge in
  \[
  W_2(C([0,T];\mathbb R^m));
  \]
- the output vector, full kernel matrix, and loss converge uniformly.

For pairwise orthogonal nonzero inputs, every assignment \(y_a\in\{\pm1\}\) produces nonzero initial residual forcing. At zero readout initialization, the initial output-kernel matrix is diagonal with strictly positive entries, so

\[
y^\top K^{(3)}(0)y>0.
\]

The lower two blocks acquire strictly positive \(t^2\) coefficients by the same adjoint-positivity argument as in the single-sample proof. Hence there is a short interval on which:

- the loss decreases;
- both hidden layers move;
- all three blocks are active;
- the kernel changes;
- the nonlinear regression residual remains positive.

Thus there are genuine multi-sample, arbitrary-label examples—but not for arbitrary input geometry.

## Final resolution

The quantifiers matter:

- “Does at least one genuine multi-sample nonlinear example exist?” — **Yes.** Pairwise orthogonal nonzero inputs with arbitrary \(\{\pm1\}\) labels give one.
- “Does such a theorem hold for every arbitrary input/label configuration?” — **No.** The all-zero-input dataset is an unconditional counterexample.
- “Does the current proof extend to every distinct, nonzero, generic dataset?” — **No known proof.** Fixed-mesh OMFP is rigorous, but continuous-time uniqueness and uniform mesh removal remain open.

Independent audits of the finite-width algebra, Tensor Program invocation, analytic stability, label cancellations, and quantifiers all reached this same three-part conclusion.

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 23377 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.318Z | assistant | final_answer ===
No. Under the stated quantifier “arbitrary input and label configuration,” the \(L=2\) conjecture is false.

In fact, one admissible two-sample dataset rules it out for every fixed non-affine \(C^2\) activation and every allowed choice of variances, layerwise learning rates, and base step sizes. The argument also closes the possible nondegenerate random-output loophole.

## Theorem

Let \(x\in\mathbb R^d\) satisfy

\[
\frac{\|x\|_2^2}{d}=1,
\]

and consider the two samples

\[
(x_1,y_1)=(x,+1),
\qquad
(x_2,y_2)=(x,-1).
\]

Use the averaged loss. Then no admissible activation and hyperparameter sequence can satisfy all the previously required properties, including:

- finite nondegenerate hidden states;
- finite uniformly convergent kernel;
- feature learning in both hidden layers;
- a joint exact-GD/gradient-flow limit;
- strict leading-order loss decrease.

This remains true even if the limiting initial output is allowed to be random.

## Proof

Because the two inputs are identical, their network outputs are identical. Write this common output as \(f_n\). Then, exactly at every width,

\[
L_n
=
\frac12\left[(f_n-1)^2+(f_n+1)^2\right]
=
1+f_n^2.
\tag{1}
\]

Consequently,

\[
\nabla_{W^{(\ell)}}L_n
=
2f_n\nabla_{W^{(\ell)}}f_n.
\tag{2}
\]

Let

\[
K_n^{(\ell)}
=
\lambda_n^{(\ell)}
\left\|\nabla_{W^{(\ell)}}f_n\right\|^2,
\qquad
K_n=\sum_{\ell=1}^3K_n^{(\ell)}.
\]

For continuous layer-scaled gradient flow,

\[
\dot f_n=-2f_nK_n.
\tag{3}
\]

We now split according to the only possible initialization scales.

### 1. Vanishing initial output

At initialization, conditionally on the first two layers,

\[
f_n(0)
=
\sum_{j=1}^n W_j^{(3)}\phi(S_j)
\]

is centered Gaussian, with conditional variance

\[
Q_n
=
\sigma_{3,n}^2
\sum_{j=1}^n\phi(S_j)^2.
\tag{4}
\]

If \(Q_n\to0\), then

\[
f_n(0)\longrightarrow0
\]

in probability. More generally, symmetry shows that every deterministic finite probability limit of \(f_n(0)\) must be zero.

Under the claimed joint gradient-flow limit, (3) passes to

\[
\dot f(t)=-2K(t)f(t),
\qquad f(0)=0.
\]

Since the required finite kernel satisfies \(K\in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}\),

\[
f(t)
=
f(0)\exp\left(-2\int_0^tK(s)\,ds\right)
=
0.
\]

Therefore

\[
L(t)=1+f(t)^2=1
\]

for every \(t\). Hence

\[
L(T)=L(0),
\]

contradicting the required strict loss decrease.

A singular discrete boundary layer cannot evade this conclusion while retaining the required vanishing discrete-GD error and convergence to a genuine gradient flow.

### 2. Nondegenerate random initial output

It remains to rule out an \(O(1)\) random Gaussian output.

Let

\[
U_i=W_i^{(1)}\cdot x,\qquad
H_i=\phi(U_i),
\]

\[
S_j=\sum_iW_{ji}^{(2)}H_i,
\qquad
G_j=\phi(S_j).
\]

The required positive finite hidden variances imply

\[
U_i\Rightarrow U\sim N(0,v_1),
\qquad
S_j\Rightarrow S\sim N(0,v_2),
\]

with \(v_1,v_2\in(0,\infty)\). Since \(\phi\) has polynomial growth,

\[
a_0:=\mathbb E\phi(U)^2\in(0,\infty).
\]

Conditionally on the first layer,

\[
\operatorname{Var}(S_j\mid U)
=
\sigma_{2,n}^2\sum_i\phi(U_i)^2.
\]

The law of large numbers and the positive limit \(v_2\) therefore give

\[
n\sigma_{2,n}^2
\longrightarrow
c_2:=\frac{v_2}{a_0}\in(0,\infty).
\tag{5}
\]

Similarly,

\[
\frac1n\sum_jG_j^2
\longrightarrow
b_0:=\mathbb E\phi(S)^2\in(0,\infty).
\]

Thus a finite nondegenerate random limit of \(f_n(0)\) is possible only if

\[
n\sigma_{3,n}^2\longrightarrow c_3\in(0,\infty).
\tag{6}
\]

We next compute the initial first-layer output-gradient norm.

Set

\[
B_i
=
\phi'(U_i)
\sum_j
W_j^{(3)}\phi'(S_j)W_{ji}^{(2)}.
\]

Then

\[
\nabla_{W_i^{(1)}}f_n=B_ix,
\]

so

\[
\left\|\nabla_{W^{(1)}}f_n\right\|_F^2
=
\|x\|^2\sum_iB_i^2.
\tag{7}
\]

The dependence between \(W_{ji}^{(2)}\) and \(S_j\) must be retained. For one row \(w\) of \(W^{(2)}\), define

\[
a_i=\phi(U_i),\qquad r_i=\phi'(U_i),
\]

\[
A=\sum_i a_i^2,\qquad
B=\sum_i r_i^2,\qquad
C=\sum_i a_i^2r_i^2,
\qquad
S=w\cdot a.
\]

Conditionally on \(U\),

\[
w=\frac{S}{A}a+\xi,
\]

where \(\xi\) is independent of \(S\) and

\[
\operatorname{Cov}(\xi)
=
\sigma_{2,n}^2
\left(I-\frac{aa^\top}{A}\right).
\]

Consequently, with \(h=\phi'\),

\[
\begin{aligned}
&\mathbb E\!\left[
h(S)^2
w^\top\operatorname{diag}(r^2)w
\mid U
\right]
\\
&=
\sigma_{2,n}^2
\left(B-\frac CA\right)
\mathbb E h(S)^2
+
\frac{C}{A^2}
\mathbb E[S^2h(S)^2].
\end{aligned}
\tag{8}
\]

Now

\[
\frac Bn\longrightarrow
a_1:=\mathbb E\phi'(U)^2,
\qquad
\frac An\longrightarrow a_0,
\]

while \(C/A=O_{\mathbb P}(1)\). From (5), the second term and the \(C/A\) correction in (8) are \(O_{\mathbb P}(n^{-1})\). Therefore the leading row contribution is

\[
c_2a_1b_1,
\qquad
b_1:=\mathbb E\phi'(S)^2.
\]

Conditioning next on \(U,W^{(2)}\), \(\sum_iB_i^2\) is a Gaussian quadratic form in \(W^{(3)}\). Row averaging and Gaussian quadratic-form concentration give

\[
\sum_iB_i^2
\longrightarrow
c_2c_3a_1b_1
\qquad\text{in probability}.
\tag{9}
\]

For completeness, concentration follows because the quadratic-form trace is \(\Theta_{\mathbb P}(n)\), while its operator norm is at most polylogarithmic in \(n\): polynomial growth gives Gaussian-max bounds for the diagonal derivative matrices, and an elementary sphere-net estimate gives

\[
\|W^{(2)}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{\mathbb P}(1)
\]

under (5). Hence the conditional variance in (9) is \(O((\log n)^q/n)\) for some finite \(q\).

Because \(\phi\) is non-affine and \(U,S\) are full-support nondegenerate Gaussians,

\[
a_1>0,\qquad b_1>0.
\]

Indeed, either expectation could vanish only if the continuous function \(\phi'\) vanished everywhere.

Combining (7) and (9),

\[
\left\|\nabla_{W^{(1)}}f_n(0)\right\|_F^2
\longrightarrow
\gamma_1
:=
\|x\|^2c_2c_3a_1b_1
>0.
\tag{10}
\]

Uniform finite convergence of the total kernel implies

\[
K_n^{(1)}(0)
=
\lambda_n^{(1)}
\left\|\nabla_{W^{(1)}}f_n(0)\right\|_F^2
\le K_n(0)
=
O_{\mathbb P}(1).
\]

Equation (10) therefore forces

\[
\lambda_n^{(1)}=O(1).
\tag{11}
\]

Now consider the polygonal interpolation of exact GD. On every step interval, let

\[
b_i=\frac{\partial f_n}{\partial U_i}.
\]

Since \(\nabla_{W_i^{(1)}}f_n=b_ix\), equations (1)–(2) give exactly

\[
\dot U_i
=
-2f_n\lambda_n^{(1)}\|x\|^2b_i.
\]

Hence

\[
\begin{aligned}
V_n^{(1)}(t)^2
&=
\frac1n\sum_i|\dot U_i|^2\\
&=
4f_n(t)^2
\frac{\lambda_n^{(1)}\|x\|^2}{n}
K_n^{(1)}(t).
\end{aligned}
\tag{12}
\]

This is an exact discrete-interpolation identity, not a Taylor or continuous-time approximation.

Using (11) and uniform compact-time boundedness of \(f_n\) and \(K_n\),

\[
\begin{aligned}
\int_0^T V_n^{(1)}(t)^2\,dt
&\le
4\|x\|^2
\frac{\lambda_n^{(1)}}n
\sup_{t\le T}|f_n(t)|^2
\int_0^TK_n^{(1)}(t)\,dt\\
&\xrightarrow{\mathbb P}0.
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus the first hidden layer is lazy, contradicting the required strictly positive feature-energy limit.

### 3. Remaining scales

If the initial output is neither vanishing nor finite nondegenerate, then it is non-tight or nonconvergent, contradicting full joint output convergence. The preceding two cases are therefore exhaustive.

## Conclusion

For the admissible normalized dataset

\[
\{(x,+1),(x,-1)\},
\]

every possible scaling fails:

- a self-averaging output gives constant limiting loss;
- a nondegenerate random output forces the first hidden layer to be lazy under a finite kernel;
- any other output scaling violates finite joint convergence.

Therefore:

\[
\boxed{\text{No single nonlinear \(L=2\) construction can work for every normalized finite dataset and every \(\{\pm1\}\)-labeling.}}
\]

This does not say that every multi-sample dataset fails. Orthogonal inputs or repeated inputs with a nonzero average label can inherit variants of the one-sample arctan construction. But the universal “arbitrary configuration” statement is definitively false. If contradictory duplicate inputs were intended to be excluded, that is a materially different conjecture requiring an explicit consistency or separation assumption.

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 24340 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.346Z | assistant | final_answer ===
The strengthened assumption removes the duplicate-label impossibility, but it does not currently yield a complete affirmative theorem. The precise resolution is:

- If the seven properties are required separately for each fixed finite dataset, the problem remains open.
- If any positive bound or convergence modulus must be uniform over all label-consistent datasets, the statement is false: distinct opposite-label inputs can approach collision and make every relevant kernel direction vanish like the squared angle.
- There is an explicit nonlinear activation and scaling that works for every such dataset at initialization and has no representational or scaling obstruction. The unresolved part is the compact-time, joint width/step-size limit for the reused random middle matrix.

Below are the proofs.

## 1. Dataset quantifier

Let

\[
D=\{(x_a,y_a)\}_{a=1}^m,\qquad
\|x_a\|^2=d,\qquad y_a\in\{-1,1\},
\]

and assume

\[
x_a=x_b\implies y_a=y_b.
\]

Identical samples with the same label can be merged, retaining their multiplicities as positive loss weights. We may therefore assume the remaining inputs are distinct.

The relevant pointwise statement is

\[
\exists(\phi,\text{scaling})\quad
\forall\text{ fixed }D,\quad
n\to\infty,\ \eta_{0,n}\to0.
\]

It does not ask for estimates uniform in the minimum input separation. A sequence of datasets \(D_n\) whose separation tends to zero is a different quantifier order.

## 2. A universal nonlinear candidate with no initialization obstruction

Take the fixed activation

\[
\phi(u)=\sin u+\cos u.
\]

It is \(C^\infty\), non-affine, and all its derivatives are bounded. Moreover, for \(G\sim N(0,1)\),

\[
\mathbb E\phi(G)^2=1.
\]

Use

\[
\operatorname{Var}(W^{(1)}_{ik})=\frac1d,\qquad
\operatorname{Var}(W^{(2)}_{ji})=\frac1n,\qquad
\operatorname{Var}(W^{(3)}_j)=\frac1{n^2},
\]

and

\[
\lambda_n^{(1)}=\frac nd,\qquad
\lambda_n^{(2)}=1,\qquad
\lambda_n^{(3)}=\frac1n,\qquad
\eta_{0,n}=n^{-2}.
\]

Then every raw step tends to zero:

\[
\eta_{0,n}\lambda_n^{(1)}=\frac1{dn},\qquad
\eta_{0,n}\lambda_n^{(2)}=\frac1{n^2},\qquad
\eta_{0,n}\lambda_n^{(3)}=\frac1{n^3}.
\]

Let

\[
R_{ab}=\frac{\langle x_a,x_b\rangle}{d}.
\]

At initialization, the first-layer preactivation vector of a typical neuron is

\[
U=(U^1,\ldots,U^m)\sim N(0,R).
\]

For any standard jointly Gaussian \(X,Y\) with correlation \(\rho\),

\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb E[\phi(X)\phi(Y)]
&=\mathbb E[\cos(X-Y)+\sin(X+Y)]\\
&=e^{\rho-1},
\end{aligned}
\]

because \(\mathbb E\sin(X+Y)=0\). The same identity holds for

\[
\phi'(u)=\cos u-\sin u.
\]

Define, entrywise,

\[
C^0_{ab}=e^{R_{ab}-1},
\qquad
C^1_{ab}=e^{C^0_{ab}-1}.
\]

Then the first postactivation Gram is \(C^0\), and the second preactivation converges to \(S\sim N(0,C^0)\). Its activation and derivative Grams both equal \(C^1\).

### Strict positive definiteness

For distinct unit vectors \(r_a=x_a/\sqrt d\),

\[
C^0_{ab}=e^{-1}e^{\langle r_a,r_b\rangle}.
\]

For any \(c\in\mathbb R^m\),

\[
c^\top C^0c
=
e^{-1}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}
\frac1{k!}
\left\|
\sum_a c_a r_a^{\otimes k}
\right\|^2.
\]

If this is zero, then

\[
\sum_a c_a r_a^{\otimes k}=0
\qquad\text{for every }k\ge0.
\]

For each \(a\), the polynomial

\[
p_a(z)=
\prod_{b\ne a}
\frac{\langle z-r_b,r_a-r_b\rangle}
{\|r_a-r_b\|^2}
\]

satisfies \(p_a(r_b)=\mathbf 1_{\{a=b\}}\). Contracting the tensor identities with the homogeneous components of \(p_a\) gives \(c_a=0\). Thus \(C^0\) is strictly positive definite.

Since \(C^0\) is a correlation matrix, it is the Gram matrix of distinct unit vectors. Applying the same argument again proves that \(C^1\) is strictly positive definite.

### All three initial kernel blocks

Define the sample kernel block

\[
K^{(\ell)}_{n,ab}
=
\lambda_n^{(\ell)}
\left\langle
\nabla_{W^{(\ell)}}f_n(x_a),
\nabla_{W^{(\ell)}}f_n(x_b)
\right\rangle .
\]

At initialization,

\[
K^{(3)}_n\xrightarrow{\mathbb P}C^1,
\]

\[
K^{(2)}_n\xrightarrow{\mathbb P}C^0\circ C^1,
\]

and

\[
K^{(1)}_n\xrightarrow{\mathbb P}R\circ C^0\circ C^1,
\]

where \(\circ\) is the Hadamard product.

The only nontrivial reuse is in \(K^{(1)}\). Writing \(C_j=nW_j^{(3)}\) and

\[
P_i^a=\sum_jW^{(2)}_{ji}C_j\phi'(S_j^a),
\]

the relevant empirical term is

\[
\frac1n\sum_i
\phi'(U_i^a)\phi'(U_i^b)P_i^aP_i^b.
\]

Conditioning first on \(W^{(1)},W^{(2)}\), Gaussian concentration of the quadratic form in \(C\) replaces \(C_jC_k\) by \(\delta_{jk}\), with conditional variance \(O(n^{-1})\). Removing column \(i\) from \(S_j\) makes \(W^{(2)}_{ji}\) independent of the remaining preactivation. Since \(\phi'\) and \(\phi''\) are bounded, restoring that column produces total \(L^1\) error bounded by

\[
\frac1n\sum_{i,j}
O\!\left(\mathbb E|W^{(2)}_{ji}|^3\right)
=O(n^{-1/2}).
\]

The remaining empirical averages converge to \(C^0_{ab}\) and \(C^1_{ab}\), giving the displayed limit.

The first two limiting blocks are strictly positive definite by the Schur product theorem. For the first block, use the following strengthening: if \(A\succeq0\) has positive diagonal and \(B\succ0\), then \(A\circ B\succ0\). Indeed, writing \(A=\sum_r u_ru_r^\top\),

\[
c^\top(A\circ B)c
=
\sum_r
(\operatorname{diag}(u_r)c)^\top
B(\operatorname{diag}(u_r)c),
\]
